<meta>This is the title page for Johann Thölde's seminal work on salt, marking the beginning of the volume.</meta>

# HALIOGRAPHIA <note>original: "Haliographia"; a title derived from Greek roots meaning 'The Description of Salt'</note>
->✤ That is: ✤<-
## **A thorough and proper description of all salt minerals**
->In which the<-

<image-desc>A horizontal row of decorative typographical fleurons.</image-desc>

# **Salt’s first matter,**
**origin, species, differences, and properties—as well as how one may test salt waters, artificially boil salt brine to profit through various methods, and refine and improve it both with and without fire—is clearly treated.**

**Together with a historical description of all saltworks, their circumstances, and locations.**

**Also how one shall extract salts from all metals and the most notable minerals, as well as from animals, herbs, and spices, and use them for human health.**

**Published for everyone, but especially for those who deal with saltworks.**

->By<-

**Johann Thölde, Hessian.** <note>Johann Thölde (c. 1565–1614) was a German chemist, salt-works official, and the likely author behind the famous alchemical works attributed to 'Basil Valentine.'</note>

->**WITH PRIVILEGE.**<- <note>original: "CUM PRIVILEGIO"; a legal notice indicating the work was published with official permission and copyright protection.</note>
->**Published by Jacob Apel, Bookseller.**<-
->**In the year 1612.**<-

<summary>The title page of Johann Thölde's 1612 treatise on salt, outlining the work's coverage of salt chemistry, industrial boiling techniques, and the medicinal extraction of salts from natural sources.</summary>
<keywords>Haliographia, salt minerals, saltworks, brine boiling, alchemy, Johann Thölde, early modern chemistry, medicinal salts</keywords>

# DEDICATION <note>original: "DEDICATORIA"</note>

implanted and left behind in His creatures; so that through certain means <note>original: "Media"; referring to the physical materials used as vehicles for healing.</note> one might come to the aid of the bodily diseases and damages that occur, and be rescued from the corruption that befalls us. # EPISTLE <note>original: "EPISTOLA"; a formal letter, often used as a dedication or preface to a book.</note>

<page-num>10</page-num>

Indeed, it should also be noted that one observes in practice <term>Proba: a practical trial, test, or experiment used to verify the effectiveness of a substance.</term> and finds that when the herbs and spices that grow in hot lands are planted in our **Germany**, they change. In their virtue, power, and operation <term>operation: the specific medicinal action or effect a substance has on the body.</term>, they take on another type <term>Genus: the category or natural kind of a thing.</term>; they are and remain thereafter never in such a quality <term>Qualitet: the inherent nature or physical characteristics of a substance, such as its balance of heat, cold, moisture, or dryness.</term> as they were before.

And the cause is that they have come under a different sky <note>original: "Himmel"; referring both to the local climate and the specific astrological environment of a region.</note> and influence <term>Influentz: the invisible power flowing from the stars and planets that was believed to shape the growth of life on Earth.</term>. And because the soil of these places is not as hot—as already mentioned—as is found in **Africa**, **Asia**, **America**, or other newly discovered hot lands and islands; so also such herbs are transmuted <term>transmutirt: transformed from one nature or substance into another.</term> into another type, and thereby receive, as mentioned, a new property.

Just like fish, when they are placed into another kind of water in which they were not yet raised <note>The text ends mid-sentence with the catchword "erzo," likely the beginning of "erzogen" (raised or bred).</note>...

Thus you are the Mother of authors, whom you, *THÖLDE*, <note>original: "THOLDE"</note>
Rescue from the shadows as wise men <note>original: "sophorum"</note>, and grant them to see the day.
Through you, *BASILIUS* <note>Referring to Basilius Valentinus, a legendary alchemical figure whose works Thölde edited and published.</note> perceives the light, with the filth removed;
You are the nourishing mother of your *BASILIUS*.
O illustrious deed! Your own age shall speak your name in verse,
And posterity shall carry you with eternal praise.
Envious is he, and he kills the man a second time,
Who desires that his writings perish forever in dark neglect.
You have a better sense, *THÖLDE*; you establish a work
Greater than its name, which previous ages did not see.
You build a work concerning *SALT* <note>original: "De SALE"</note> with much salt <note>A Latin pun: *sal* refers to physical salt, but also to "wit" or intellectual brilliance.</note>, and you reveal
The springs and teach what the origin of salts may be.
You enumerate how many salt-works <note>original: "Salinas"</note> the German land cherishes,
Whether the salt comes in streams or is dug from the fields.
By what art <term>Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire, representing the intense heat required to boil brine into salt.</note></term> prepares the hearths, or how he enriches
The salt-works by the blowing of the winds and cooks with fire.
But a work greater than these rises: from the deepest recesses
You draw out the salts of things by a wondrous art.
Salt holds the <term>Arcadian youth <note>original: "Arcadicum juvenem"; a poetic reference to Mercury (Hermes), representing the alchemical principle of Mercury.</note></term>; salt mixed with sulfur,
The bond of nature, fosters every single thing in the world.
Be blessed in your spirit, *THÖLDE*, to bring forth similar
Offspring hereafter; thus shall posterity carry you to the high stars.

## ON THE DESCRIPTION OF SALT
<note>original: "IN HALIGRAPHIAM." The title uses a Greek-derived Latin term: *hals* (salt) and *graphia* (writing or description).</note>

### Concerning the Honorable, Respectable, and Very Wise Mr. Johann Thölde, Chamberlain of the Council at Frankenhausen, and Master of the Salt Pans <term>Master of the Salt Pans <note>original: "Pfannenherrns." A title for a high-ranking official or owner of the boiling pans in a salt-works. In salt-producing towns like Frankenhausen, this was a position of great wealth and political influence.</note></term> of the highly useful salt-works located there,
->My old acquaintance and very good friend.<-

I write this in brief:
Salt is the very best spice;
Salt is a noble creature;
Salt was brought forth by Divine creation
For the good of all that lives and moves,
Even if it lies deep within the earth,
In rocks, mountains, and in stones,
In seawater and pure wells.
It is a balm for all things,
Which God created from the beginning
For health and medicine. <note>original: "Artzeney."</note>
Therefore, O Lord Christ, stand by me,
That through the salt of Your wisdom,
I may write poetry concerning Your creation.
Salt originally takes its name
In Holy Scripture from its deed [or effect]...
<note>The poet is likely referring to the biblical "Salt of the Earth" or the use of salt in sacrifices, suggesting that the word "salt" implies preservation and purity.</note>

<meta>catchword: Because [original: "Weils"]</meta>

<summary>This page features the opening of a German poem dedicated to Johann Thölde, framing salt as a divine creation essential for both health and daily life. It emphasizes salt's omnipresence in nature—found in mountains, rocks, and water—and its status as a "noble balsam."</summary>
<keywords>Johann Thölde, Frankenhausen, salt production, Master of the Salt Pans, medicinal salt, divine creation, German poetry, salt-works</keywords>

Because it bites, is sharp, and lets nothing rot,
Therefore the Scripture firmly calls its promise
A holy covenant of salt, <note>original: "Saltzbund." Referring to the biblical "covenant of salt" (as in Numbers 18:19 or 2 Chronicles 13:5), signifying a permanent, incorruptible, and binding agreement.</note>
As one knows from the Bible.
The Greeks [obtained salt] entirely from seawater
There, in the brilliance of the sun;
Therefore the sea and the salt received
One name, as I have heard. <note>The poet is referencing the Greek word *hals* (ἅλς), which means both "salt" and "sea."</note>
Even today in German lands,
Salt is called a "Hall" in those places; <term>Hall <note>A historic term for a salt-works or a place where salt is produced. Many German cities, such as Halle an der Saale or Hallstatt, derive their names from this root.</note></term>
The wells of salt-flow in Latin
Are called purely "Salen," <note>original: "Salen." A reference to the river Saale, whose name is linguistically linked to the Latin *sal* (salt).</note>
Because upon the Saale river
Salt wells are found in great abundance.
Hungary and Poland provide rock salt;
The Inn Valley <note>original: "Inthal." Likely the Inntal in the Tyrol region, famous for the Hall in Tirol salt mine.</note> consumes "writing salt"; <note>original: "Schreibensaltz." This likely refers to a specific grade of fine-grained, high-quality refined salt, perhaps used in industrial processes or designated for specific trade.</note>
The cities of Halle and Frankenhausen, indeed,
Make use of the clear wells,
And many more places that I do not know,
And therefore I do not name them.
Christ says: Salt is not a lowly thing,
But a glorious and good thing. <note>A reference to Mark 9:50: "Salt is good; but if the salt has lost its saltness, wherewith will ye season it?"</note>
Since I cannot describe to everyone
The many uses of salt,
Which people use in medicine,
Along with livestock and artisans as well,

<sig>B v</sig>
<meta>catchword: Like</meta>

<image-desc>A horizontal decorative border composed of repeating typographical ornaments with floral motifs at the top of the text block.</image-desc>

Enjoying the noble salt (or lard), <note>The original German rhymes "Saltz" (salt) with "Schmalz" (rendered fat or lard), suggesting both were essential pantry staples.</note>
On herring, stockfish, meat, and fish—
And if a householder lacks it in his home,
He soon sends someone out to buy it.
All cheese, butter, milk, and herbs, <note>original: "Kraut." While this can mean "cabbage" (as in sauerkraut), in this context it refers more broadly to vegetables and greens preserved with salt.</note>
Along with everything that has flesh and skin,
Is kept well by the salt,
For which many people long.
Because salt is, without any mockery, <note>original: "ohn allen spott." A common 16th-century idiom meaning "verily" or "without a doubt."</note>
Such a noble creation of God
That no one can do without it,
Let everyone thank God for it;
For if it were not there, everything would quickly rot—
Whatever lives, whatever soars, whatever it may be,
In earth, in water, in air, in fire—
Wherever salt does not come to the rescue.
And where, next to God, through artful boiling <term>boiling <note>original: "sieden." This refers to the industrial process of "brine boiling," where salt-water was heated in large pans to crystallize the salt.</note></term>
The alchemist <note>In this context, "alchemist" refers to the salt-maker or technical chemist who understands the "art" of refining minerals, rather than someone trying to turn lead into gold.</note> here on earth
Had not discovered the salt, dear people,
Such is truly a good prize,
Through which a person in these times
Maintains their health against sickness;
Because salt can also preserve a dead body,
Whether of man or woman,
Against decay and worms, through the power
That God Himself has instilled within it,

How much more can it preserve the body
Which lives, and moves, and soars;
To obtain such knowledge now,
I advise that you should read
Most diligently the *Haligraphia*, <note>A seminal work on salt production published in 1603. The title combines the Greek words for "salt" (hals) and "writing" (graphia).</note>
Of the praiseworthy Johann Thölde, <note>Johann Thölde (c. 1565–1614) was a chemist, salt-works official, and editor. He is widely believed to be the actual author behind the alchemical works of the pseudonymous "Basil Valentine."</note>
In which he has described salt;
There you will clearly find it all:
What its origin and primary root may be.
You need not tumble far across the land for it—
Its nature, character, and properties,
Its distinctions, its types, and its power—
He has described it all quite finely.
Also, how one should purely test
Salt-water, and what is good within it,
To learn for certain what it contains.
The salt-boiling <term>salt-boiling <note>original: "Siederwerck." This refers to the industrial infrastructure of a saltworks where brine is boiled in large pans to crystallize the salt.</note></term> is very finely described
In many ways along the Saale <note>The Saale is a river in central Germany. The city of Halle, located on its banks, was one of the most important salt-producing centers in Europe for centuries.</note> down below:
By heather, wood, reeds, straw, stone-
->coals<-
Through thorn-bushes, <note>original: "Dorff," likely a variant of "Dornen" (thorns). This refers to the "graduation" process where brine was trickled over stacks of brushwood to evaporate water and increase the salt concentration before boiling.</note> and finally how the brines <term>brines <note>original: "Solen." Natural salt-water springs or highly concentrated salt solutions.</note></term>
Are enriched and improved without fire,
Even if at first they are quite watery.
The circumstances and the layout
Of established saltworks are also shown.

->ANOTHER<- <note>original: "ALIUD." A common Latin heading used in manuscripts and early printed books to indicate an additional poem or epigram on the same subject.</note>

All things created by God in the whole world are good,
Yet nothing is more useful than the Sun and Salt. <note>original: "Utile Sole tamē nil magis atq; Sale." This aphorism highlights the two essentials for life: the Sun (energy/spirit) and Salt (matter/preservation).</note>
By Phoebus <note>A poetic name for the Sun god in classical mythology.</note> all things thrive as he hurls rays of heat,
When he sets, the highest light of day fades.
The very balsam of Salt wards off the decay of rot,
Properly keeping preserved things whole for a long time.
Finally, of those things which constitute the wide world,
Salt is also one of the three principles. <term>three principles <note>original: "tria principia." In the "Spagyric" or Paracelsian alchemy of this era, all matter was believed to be composed of three fundamental substances: Salt (body/stability), Sulfur (soul/inflammability), and Mercury (spirit/volatility).</note></term>
Therefore, everything contains its own Salt, and possesses its own
Balsamic liquid, by which it protects itself.
Finally, if I must explain the great mysteries of the ancient Sages
Which the <term>Spagyrists <note>original: "Spagyri." Alchemists who followed the methods of Paracelsus, focusing on the separation and recombination of natural elements to create medicine.</note></term> deal with:
They entitle Gold with the name of the Heavenly Sun,
And very great powers are attributed to it,

<summary>This page concludes a poetic endorsement of salt's medicinal extraction from various natural sources and begins a Latin epigram by the physician Sebastian Greiff. Yet you will achieve nothing, whether you fume with ground-up powder
    Or even desire to drink it in your own way;
Unless you have the inner parts opened and made firm by a key,
    A harmless one, such as is known to exist in Salt. <note>original: "clavi... Sali." In alchemical literature, a "key" is a secret process or substance—here Salt—used to "unlock" or dissolve metals and minerals to reveal their medicinal essence.</note>
This key you reveal here, O **JOHANNES**, so greatly beloved by the Muses, <note>Referring to the author/editor Johann Thölde.</note>
    And you allow no room for envy to take hold.
It is therefore right to offer you great thanks, because
    You wish for us to share in the knowledge of this Book.
Continue to publish in a similar manner the many things you preserve,
    And your name will be dear to us for a long time.

->A N O T H E R<- <note>original: "ALIUD." A standard heading indicating another poem on the same theme.</note>

**Y**ou bring forth **SALT**, Thölde, from the storehouse for mortals,
    Therefore the cook prepares the seasonings, and then Health <note>original: "Hygiéa." The Greek goddess of health and cleanliness.</note> appears.
Be not a mere hoarder, hiding away the monuments of **BASIL**,
    But be a steward, so that the storehouse of Basil may lie open. <note>original: "BASILII." This refers to Basil Valentine, a legendary 15th-century alchemist. Thölde claimed to have found Valentine’s hidden manuscripts behind a pillar in the Erfurt cathedral.</note>
The more the writings of **BASIL** are now read by the learned,
    The greater your glory, **THÖLDE**, shall be.

    **THÖLDE**, of all the salt-works found throughout the world:

    Finding fault, yet it shines vibrantly in the light of this age.

# Preface /

## To the Kind-hearted Reader / at the Opening of This Book.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'G' at the beginning of the text, featuring scrolling foliate designs.</image-desc>

Kind-hearted reader of this book: Just as the everlasting Creator <term>Schöpffer; God as the ultimate architect of nature</term> in the beginning created heaven and earth, and [created] man in His image <term>Ebenbilde; the concept of humans being made in the likeness of God</term> with a rational soul, He also endowed the earth with various and countless blessings and benefits <note>original: "beneficien vnd wolthaten". The author uses both a Latin-derived term and a traditional German word to emphasize the abundance of divine gifts.</note> for the maintenance of this temporal life. He stocked the forests with many species of animals; He arranged the fish in the sea and other waters; He adorned the air with birds, which have their flight and dwelling therein; He sowed the fields with herbs and all kinds of fruits; and He also planted the types of metals and minerals into the mountains.

<note>Vocabulary: Creator, Image/Likeness, blessings/benefits, Metals, Minerals</note>

<summary>This preface introduces the book by framing the study of nature and chemistry as a recognition of God's divine providence, specifically noting that minerals and metals were placed in the earth for human benefit.</summary>
<keywords>preface, Creator, creation, providence, minerals, metals, soul, natural philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's preface to the reader. Page 30 concluded by listing the various components of creation—animals, fish, birds, herbs, and minerals—that God provided. # To the Kind-hearted Reader.

All for the purpose that man should be a lord and ruler over these things, and should use them for food and the sustenance of his life, for the clothing of his body, and for the recovery and preservation of his health. For this, we weak creatures should rightly thank the Highest <term>Höchste; a common title for God</term> every hour and moment for His glorious, mild, and rich grace, which He has so fatherly shown and shared with us.

Since, then, among these recounted gifts of God, it is not the least gift and benefit, but is instead to be recognized and found among the most principal, that among other minerals He has also provided us humans with rich salt mines, springs, ores, and veins <term>Gängen; geological lodes or pathways in the earth where minerals are deposited</term> of the same, providing for our needs and sharing them richly. From these, we receive and partake in the noblest spice of salt, which we humans cannot do without <note>In the early modern period, salt was viewed not just as a seasoning, but as a vital "balm" for the body and a fundamental chemical principle necessary for all life.</note>; which through

# Preface to the
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

Which is now to be understood in this way: just as all sacrifices in the Old Testament had to be salted, and a portion of every sacrifice was burned with fire, there is now a spiritual interpretation—namely, that the "Old Man" <note>original: *alte Mensch*; a theological term from the Apostle Paul referring to the unregenerate, sinful nature of humanity</note> is indeed crucified and consumed as if by a burning fire and sharp salt, as Paul says in Romans 12. For our body is the true salt; but where the salt now becomes flavorless <note>original: *thum*; an archaic term for salt losing its potency or savor, rendering it useless</note>—that is, when the preachers cease to teach—maggots then grow. By this, it is meant that false doctrine and error are introduced, the Word is corrupted, and a godless life follows thereafter. Salt stings <note>original: *beißet*; literally "bites," referring to the sharp, purifying pain of salt on a wound</note>, but for that reason, it is necessary that within the salt—that is, when the Cross, tribulation, and persecution come—one has peace and patience.

The fact that salt was also commanded to be used for all sacrifices signifies this: that everything we do should be done to the honor of the **Lord** Christ, and for

The ancient historian **Herodotus** <note>Herodotus (c. 484–425 BC) was a Greek historian known for recording the geography and customs of the ancient world</note> provides various distinct reports and accounts of **salt mines** <term>salt mines: original "Saltzbergwercken," referring to the excavation of rock salt from the earth rather than evaporating it from water</term>, where he specifically notes that several miles beyond **Armenia**, and not very far from **Egileta**, saltworks are said to be located.

Likewise, where the people of **Atlantis** <note>original: *Atlantis*; in this context, Herodotus was likely referring to the "Atlantes" people of North Africa near the Atlas Mountains, rather than the legendary sunken city</note> were found, living a ten-day journey from the **Garamantes** <note>An ancient civilization in modern-day Libya known for their advanced underground irrigation systems</note>. This salt mine is said to extend very far, and large pieces are quarried there, with which the inhabitants are said to have built their houses; this is possible because in that part of **Africa** there is constant, intense heat, and rain is never felt.

**Ancus Marcius** <note>original: *Arcus Martius*; the legendary fourth King of Rome, credited with establishing the first Roman salt-pans at Ostia</note> is said to have been the first who originally discovered and built the saltworks in **Rome**, as **Pliny** states in *Book 31, Chapter 7* <note>original: *Plinius libr. 31. cap. 7.*; referring to Pliny the Elder’s encyclopedic "Natural History"</note>.

# To the [kind-hearted reader]

# The First Part

Initially, my dear reader, you should know that the word **S A L** or Salt generally derives its name from the Greek word *hals* <note>original Greek: ἅλς</note>, as a substance that we use in the food we eat. From the Hebrews, salt receives its name from its "sharpness" <note>The author likely refers to the Hebrew word *melach*, though here he emphasizes the quality of sharpness or pungency</note>, for it is this sharpness that preserves all things from decay.

Regarding the origin, beginning, and the first **matter** of the nature of salt, however, some have believed that the salt springs arise from the great sea, and penetrate far into the depths through the earth via special hidden crevices and caves, and then find an outlet to break forth. This opinion and view, however, is very far from the truth, for these reasons: it is well known that the salty sea is very low in salt content, and generally is not found to hold more than two, three, or four *Lot* <term>Lot: a historical German unit of weight; in this context, it measures the concentration of salt per unit of water</term> in the weight of salt. On the other hand, salt springs are found that contain over sixteen, eighteen, or even up to twenty-odd *Lot*—a concentration which the sea possesses in no place.

<summary>The author explains the etymological origins of the word "salt" and argues that inland salt springs cannot originate from the sea because their salt concentration is significantly higher than that of ocean water.</summary>
<keywords>etymology, Greek, Hebrew, salt springs, salinity, Lot weight, geological theories</keywords>

### Of Salt Minerals

Others are of a different opinion and argue that one finds large, massive salt deposits <term>salt deposits: original *Saltzstöcke*, referring to large subterranean masses of rock salt</term>, as they call them, in the great depths of the earth. They believe that waters flow through these, washing the salt away and carrying it out to the surface. If this were indeed true and certain, the logical consequence <note>original: *Consequentia*</note> would follow that over so many thousands or hundreds of years, such a grown salt deposit would have been entirely washed away and carried out by the water. Even if it had been many miles in size, the springs that previously cast out salt would now carry nothing more with them.

Therefore, this reasoning <note>original: *Ratio*</note> has no standing. Even if such a salt mountain might lie hidden in the earth—as is found in India, Poland, and the Inn Valley <note>original: *Inthal*; likely referring to the salt mines of Hall in the Inn Valley, Tyrol</note>—it is merely material <note>original: *Materialisch*; referring to the physical state of the substance rather than its energetic or "spiritual" origin</note> and is not the "first root and seed" of the salt. It would become far too long and rambling here—and perhaps cause the reader some annoyance—to recount the many various opinions regarding the matter of salt.

### The First Part

# of the Salt-Minerals.

This **spirit of salt** <term>spirit of salt: an acidic distillate, typically hydrochloric acid, produced from mineral salts</term> brings to metals a **fixity** <term>fixity: the chemical property of being stable in heat and resistant to vaporization</term>; it makes them heavy and compact. Therefore, this salt is not at all suitable for food, but remains a salt for itself until further investigation.

A salt is used that is called **alkali salt** <note>original: *Sal Alkali*</note>, which is very well known to goldsmiths and is used as a **flux** <note>original: *Floß*</note> for metals. But because this salt is a **compound** <note>original: *Compositum*</note>, it is also not a salt **in its own right** <note>original: *per se*</note>; rather, it is an **alkali** that has been separated and held back from other things, as the **soap-boilers** <note>original: *Seiffensieder*</note> have experienced. It **dissolves** <note>original: *solviret*</note> quickly and soon **coagulates** <note>original: *coagulirt*</note> again. Therefore, one cannot describe it as a unique salt, for its **primary essence** <note>original: *primum ens*</note> is not of itself, but has been united from common **table salt**, **ashes**, and **lime**; thus it becomes

## The Fourth Chapter

### Briefly demonstrates that no salt can be dispensed with for the preservation of the human body.

Everyone knows that one cannot do without salt for the preservation of the human body. For without salt, neither humans nor animals can actually live, nor be kept alive; for all humans sustain themselves through salt, and salt is a **preservative** <term>preservative: *Præservatiu* — in early medicine, a substance used to guard the body against disease and corruption</term> against the **ailments** <note>original: *Zufälle* — literally "accidents," referring to the sudden onset of symptoms or medical complications</note> that lead to **putrefaction** <term>putrefaction: *fäulung* — the biological decay or rotting of organic matter, which early modern physicians believed was a primary cause of disease</term>. Animals also use salt, and by their very nature are quite eager for it for their own preservation. The fish in the water enjoy the salt which is carried to them through various waters, even if the quantity is often so small that one cannot even sense it. In addition, every fish has its natural salt within it—

<meta>catchword: ange- [likely *angeboren*, meaning innate or inborn]</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Chapter Four, arguing that salt is a fundamental necessity for all life—human, animal, and aquatic—acting as a vital preservative against biological decay.</summary>
<keywords>preservation of life, biology of salt, putrefaction, animal health, aquatic salt, medicinal salt</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter Four regarding the biological necessity of salt. It concludes the chapter by arguing that even when salt is not visible, it exists as a "spirit" or "essence" within all living things, sustained by celestial influences.</meta>

<header>of Salt-Minerals.</header>

innate / in its own blood, which is preserved, fed, and nourished by the **spirit of salt** <term>spirit of salt: *Salßgeist* — in alchemy, the essential or volatile principle of salt; here it refers to a life-sustaining essence rather than a specific chemical acid</term> which comes to them in the water. And although many animals are found that do not use visible salt, they are nonetheless preserved and nourished through other food which possesses a hidden salty nature within it. In summary, there is no living creature in the world that can dispense with salt; if it does not live from the salt that comes to it in and through its food, it nevertheless nourishes and maintains its flesh from its own spirit of salt, which is assigned to it through the **stars** <note>original: *Sydera* — referring to the celestial or astrological influences believed to govern the growth and composition of earthly matter</note>.

Since this chapter is more useful for the **preservation of health** <note>original: *ad conservationem valetudinis*</note> than for other circumstances, I will hereby conclude this chapter, even though many **arguments** <note>original: *Argumenta*</note> could be introduced as evidence, so that a consistent execution and certain **proof** <term>proof: *Proba* — a trial, test, or definitive demonstration of a theory's truth</term> of this chapter could be demonstrated and shown.

# The Second Part

salt-water is recorded, and the weight of the vessel in which the salt-water was weighed is subtracted from the previous weight. The salt-water is then placed into an iron or lead pan and set upon a coal fire until all the water has evaporated and the salt remains lying dry.

Collect this dry salt together, take it out, and weigh it by **loths** <term>loth: *Loht*—a traditional German unit of weight, usually 1/32nd of a pound</term>; divide the loths into pounds and the pounds into **hundredweights** <term>hundredweight: *Centner*—a unit of weight typically equal to 100 or 110 pounds</term>. In this **manner** <note>original: *Modum*</note>, you can quickly calculate how many loths or pounds are found in a hundredweight of salt **brine** <term>brine: *Sole*—naturally occurring salt water</term>. For in a salt **assay** <term>assay: *Salzproba*—a trial or test to determine the mineral content of a sample</term>, a loth and a pound are treated as equivalent units of proportion; whether one speaks in terms of large or small weights does not matter. After you have measured the **brine** <note>original: *Sola*</note> that you have tested, you can then easily calculate how many of these measures make a **stübchen** <term>stübchen: *Stübichen*—a liquid measure, roughly 3.7 liters</term>, how many stübchens make an **eymer** <term>eymer: *Eymer*—a bucket or cask</term> or **ohm** <term>ohm: *Ohm*—a standard liquid measure, often around 140 liters</term>, and how many ohms or eymers a **fuder** <term>fuder: *Fuder*—a large cartload or vat</term> contains, and how many hundredweights [of salt]...

Then I let the aforementioned **probing spindle** <term>probing spindle: *Probirspindel*—an early hydrometer used to measure the density of liquids</term> sink into it without any salt content. In this way, you can proceed continually from one lot **up to the highest degree** <note>original Latin: *usque ad summum gradum*</note>. The markings for the salt content can then be observed and noted from one level to the next, for this **assay** <term>assay: *Proba*—a technical test to determine the salt content of brine</term> is calibrated according to the precision and fixed divisions of inches and the **measuring rod** <term>measuring rod: *Meßstabes*—a standardized scale or ruler</term>, which do not fail. It must also be observed and diligently noted that the pans and hearths <term>hearths: *Herde*—the combustion chambers and structural supports beneath the boiling pans</term> are so coordinated <note>original: *concordiren*—to harmonize or match in design</note> that the heat and flame of the fire are not dampened or suppressed, but can achieve their free draft and combustion.

Our ancestors without doubt used more powerful fueling systems <term>fueling systems: *Feuerwerck*—the specific arrangement of fuel and furnaces to generate heat</term> in certain places than can be used today, and they designed their pans and hearths accordingly. Yet the pans and hearths are still used just as they were by the ancients. <meta>This page continues the description from page 87 regarding the maintenance of salt-boiling furnaces. The previous page ended with the workers hacking out the mineralized crust (schlotter) from the hearth using picks.</meta>

->### The Second Part<-

...[hacked] out, it is placed on a **grate** <note>original: *Hord*—a wooden rack or framework used for drainage or filtering</note>, where one pours fresh **brine**—just as it comes from the well—into a **vat** or trough. This brine pulls the salt back out of the <term>schlotter: the mineral-rich crust or sediment that builds up on the hearth</term> and enriches itself; the masters call this **gebeiß** <note>literally "the bitten"; refers to the leached brine or the process of "biting" the salt out of the sediment</note>. Many people, indeed the majority, consider this to be of great benefit, but when one reflects upon it correctly, the profit gained thereby is very small. For if much moisture gets under the pan, then all the more **fuel** <note>original: *Fewerwerck*—literally "fire-work," referring to the firewood and the labor of tending the furnace</note> must be used so that the hearths may be dried out again; thus, every bit of enriched brine must first be forced out by the cost of the fuel.

This leached brine never yields salt as good, pure, beautiful, clear, and light as the unleached brine does; instead, it becomes much heavier and more compact, settles together strongly, and cannot be measured as easily as the unleached salt. However, whoever buys this **gebeiß** salt for their household loses nothing by it; rather, it brings them more than less <note>The author suggests that because the salt is denser, a buyer receives more actual salt by weight even if the volume looks the same</note>. And let this be enough regarding the manner of boiling for this time, and herewith this chapter is concluded.

**Vocabulary:** Brine, Salt, Sediment-Crust, Leached-Brine, Fuel/Firing, Boiling, Vats, Grate.

<summary>This page describes the "gebeiß" process of reclaiming salt from furnace sediment by leaching it with fresh brine. While a common practice, the author argues it is inefficient due to the extra fuel required to dry the furnace hearths afterward.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the previous chapter's conclusion regarding the boiling process and the use of leached brine. # Of Salt-Minerals. #

How the <term>salt</term> in the final boiling is produced in many ways—in pieces, in whole masses, or packed into baskets—will be partly mentioned in the third part of this book, where each <term>salt-works</term> is described in detail <note>original: *in specie*—specifically or individually</note>. For one salt is not struck out and produced in the same manner as another. Some are produced by open flames, fire, or blaze, while others are produced by the coals that are burned in the hearth.

From this it also follows that, due to such circumstances, one person can produce more and another less from the same quantity of finished <term>brine: saltwater prepared for boiling</term>, as far as the measurement of the volume is concerned. For the greater the heat used during the extraction, the smaller the grain of the salt becomes. However, the cooler the heat—as is the case with coal fires—the more subtly it behaves during the <term>simmering: the slow seething process of salt crystallization; original: *söken*</term>, and it acquires a coarse grain. Yet, moderation <note>original: *masse*—here meaning "measure" or "moderation"</note> is good in all things, so that the seller and the buyer may both remain satisfied <note>The author explains that coarse salt and fine salt have different volumes for the same weight; if the grain is too fine or too coarse, one party in a transaction might feel cheated by the volume-to-weight ratio</note>.

One <term>brine</term> also does not boil exactly like another, for one always has this nature and the other another nature, according to which they...

<meta>catchword: ...others</meta>

It emphasizes the importance of consistent salt quality for fair trade between sellers and buyers.</summary>

## The Second Part ##

...which brings other <term>qualities: original: *Munera*—natural gifts or properties</term> with it. Many types of <term>brine: saltwater prepared for boiling</term> are found that will not turn into salt if any fat or grease enters the boiling process; conversely, some workers actually use fat [as an additive]. Many use egg whites for the <term>scouring: the process of removing impurities; original: *abscheurung*</term> and clarification of impure brine. Likewise, in some places resin is added to the brine; in others, soot, beer, or sulfur, and various other things. 

Much also depends on skilled, well-practiced, and experienced masters, who differ greatly from one another; for when a problem arises, they must know how to provide counsel and a remedy. One master also has a lighter hand in the work than another. Many strike and beat the salt so heavily and densely together that the excess <term>finished brine: the liquid remaining after crystallization; original: *Gare Sole*</term> cannot drain out of the salt-cakes. This results in little profit for the lords who must provide the <term>fuel: original: *Fewerwerck*—literally "fire-work," referring here to the wood and resources for the furnace</term>. Therefore, not all are cooks who carry long knives <note>This is a traditional German proverb meaning that possessing the tools of a trade does not make one a true expert in it.</note>.

Indeed, many have also attempted to extract the salt from the brine without any boiling...

## The Third Chapter

# That salt can also be boiled and made
# with coal, peat, and heather,
# just as with wood, reeds, and straw.

That salt is boiled with <term>coal: specifically stone coal or mineral coal; original: *Steinkolen*</term>, <term>peat: an accumulation of partially decayed vegetation; original: *Dorff*</term>, and <term>heather: original: *Heyden*</term>, just as it is with wood, reeds, and straw, requires no proof, since the practice has proven itself through its own application. Peat is used extensively in the Netherlands as <term>fuel: material burned to produce heat; original: *Fewerwerck*</term>, and especially at Sulza in the Lüneburg region <note>The Lüneburg region in northern Germany was a primary center for salt production for centuries due to its massive salt domes.</note> for boiling salt, just as heather is also used for boiling at Lüder in the <term>Abbey of Fulda: a sovereign ecclesiastical principality; original: *Stifft Fulda*</term>.

However, so that the inexperienced may be shown what kind of <term>matter: original: *materia*</term> peat is, you should know that it is a dug-up earth permeated with many roots. It is dug up in specific places where it grows, and then withered or dried before being used as fuel. That which is dug up, however...

<summary>This chapter explains that salt production is not limited to wood fuel, noting the use of coal, peat, and heather in various regions like Lüneburg and Fulda. It provides a basic definition of peat for readers unfamiliar with the fuel source.</summary>

A **structure** <term>Gebew; here referring to the framework of a graduation tower</term> is erected, narrow and long, the compartments of which are not walled in or plastered <note>original: *gekleibet*; referring to the use of wattle and daub or clay to seal walls</note>, so that the air can easily blow through the structure. <meta>This page continues the description of the salt-making process, specifically detailing how brine is moved through a series of basins to increase its concentration and the physical materials used to facilitate evaporation.</meta>

# The Second Part

**Brine** is released for **gradation** <term>Gradation; the process of increasing salt concentration through natural evaporation</term>. Several such basins are manufactured and set up one after another. When the brine in one basin has become sufficiently enriched, it is drained into another basin, because each basin is positioned and set somewhat higher than the next. This exchange is repeated until the brine is rich enough in salt to be used for **boiling** <term>sieden; the final stage of salt production where brine is heated in pans to crystalize the salt</term>. 

When some has been boiled off from the first basin, the next follows after it. New brine is then let into the first basin again to join the previous enriched brine as a reserve, so that no disruption can occur. Thus, through the quantity of basins, the advancement of many **boilings** <term>Soden; a single cycle or "batch" of salt-boiling</term> can be achieved.

However, this advancement of the **trickling** <term>leckens; the process of brine dripping over surfaces to maximize air exposure</term> cannot happen without **reeds** <term>schelff; likely sedge or marsh reeds</term> and **straw**, which are woven together in the proper manner and hung along the length of the entire structure above the basins, just like **sides of bacon** <note>original: *Speckseiten*; a vivid comparison to slabs of cured meat hanging in a smokehouse</note>. Accordingly, special **tricklers** <term>Lecker; specialized laborers who managed the flow of brine over the evaporation surfaces</term> are kept on a specific weekly wage, who

There is, however, another kind of **grading** <term>Gradierung; the process of concentrating a mineral solution by evaporating the water content</term>, which is done through the air without **fire**. Because it is not easily scaled up to a large quantity or built for massive production, such grading is most useful for those who deal with the boiling of **vitriol** <term>Victriol; a group of sulfate minerals used in tanning, dyeing, and medicine</term>, **alum**, and **saltpeter**, to concentrate their **lyes** <term>Laugen; the liquid solutions containing these minerals</term> and to save on the cost of firewood.

A jointed, open **basin** <term>Kaste; a large wooden vat</term> is placed inside; over the basin, within the open building, a wide wheel is hung, almost as wide as the entire basin. This wheel is entirely empty and hollow in the middle; it is woven through and closed up with **reeds** <term>Schelff; likely marsh reeds or sedge</term>, straw, or **smooth bark** <note>original: *geschliffen Past*; likely referring to bast or inner bark fibers used for weaving</note>. ### The Fifth Chapter.
## On the Remarkable Shape and Crystallization of Salt.

Depending on how the fire is managed—whether **moderately** <note>original: *modicè*</note> or with great heat—the salt **crystallizes** <term>Anschiessung; the process of salt crystals forming and "shooting" out of the solution as it evaporates</term> into its own specific form and shape. The cooler the salt is extracted, the coarser its **grain** <term>Saltzkorn; the individual crystal size</term> becomes. This does not happen during high-heat extraction; instead, the grain becomes so small that its shape and form cannot be judged as easily as when the salt grain is coarse. In the latter case, one sees with wonder—

...ing, how the salt has **crystallized** <term>anschiessen; the process where salt crystals "shoot" or form out of a liquid</term>; for it appears triangular, square, pentagonal, and hexagonal, as if it had been diligently divided with a **compass** <term>Circkel; a geometric drawing tool used for precision</term> and sharply cut on all sides, like **precious stones** <note>original: *Edlengesteine*</note>, or especially like pointed, faceted diamonds. One also occasionally finds wonderful, distinct shapes like true natural stars, appearing like a crystal, from which one can perceive and notice what a unique form, shape, and essence the **Creator** has placed into the **mineral of the earth** <term>Minera; the raw mineral ore or substance found underground</term>, causing us to marvel at the nature infused within it.

Just as common salt visibly proves its infused, active form in its crystallization, so too do other salts do the same according to their kind. **Saltpeter** <note>Also known as nitre; a key ingredient in gunpowder and medicine</note> also crystallizes after it has been boiled and purified—at times in small particles like feathers, and at other times with beautiful, large, transparent rays like a crystal (which, if one does not <unclear>suspect</unclear> fraud or adulteration...

### Of Salt-Minerals.

...shamed / <note>original: *schemet*; likely referring to the "shameful" practice of adulteration mentioned on the previous page</note> otherwise, these are also easily provided and made with the addition of **alum** <term>Alaun; a mineral salt used as a mordant in dyeing and for its astringent properties</term>.) It also **crystallizes** <term>anschiessen; the chemical process where crystals "shoot" or form out of a liquid solution</term> moderately. This is of no great concern, provided it has been properly and thoroughly separated from impurities and its **dross** <term>Schlacke; the waste matter or impure residue left over from refining</term>. You can find the **proof** <term>Proba; a technical test or assay to determine purity</term> of this if, during its ignition and burning, it does not leave much **dead matter** <note>Inert, non-reactive residue that remains after a chemical reaction</note> sitting and lying behind.

Likewise, **vitriol** <term>Victriol; a group of sulfate minerals, such as copper or iron sulfate, used in many industrial and medicinal processes</term> has its own manner of crystallizing, both coarse and fine, according to how it is handled in the fire. So too does **alum**, which however remains and shows itself especially in a certain shape of its pieces during crystallization, if it is treated correctly during **boiling or refining** <term>leutern; the process of purifying a substance by boiling it and removing impurities</term>, so that the **finished batch** <term>Gare; the precise state of "doneness" or readiness in chemical refining</term> is not boiled too thick. The masters, as well as those who boil **saltpeter** and other salts, can quickly see and observe this by their **test**, which some perform on an iron, while others

# The Third Part of this Book/

## Wherein an account is given of all the most prominent salt springs or salt wells of the German lands, together with the situation of the saltworks / 

->And beginning with the most magnificent and richest salt spring at Lüneburg.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut drop cap 'L' featuring a pastoral landscape scene with hills and a tree.</image-desc>
Lüneburg, a city in Lower Saxony, very well known; there, a salt spring rises almost in the middle of the city. This well pours forth a magnificent, beautiful, pure, and clear **brine** <term>Sole; naturally occurring salt water</term> from itself, which is not notably mixed with any other minerals. This salt brine is conducted underground for approximately one hundred paces to the **saltworks** <term>Saltzwerck; the industrial facility where salt is extracted from brine</term>, where it enters a well which is lined in a circle with strong floorboards <note>The German "gespundet" suggests the boards were joined with tongue-and-groove precision to make the well-casing watertight</note>, and from which it was drawn in ancient times with a large **sweep-arm** <term>Schwengel; a long, counterweighted lever used to lift water buckets</term>. Now, however, the brine is raised by **pumping machinery** <term>Pompenwerck; a more modern mechanical system for drawing water</term>,

The brine holds a measure of one hundred and eight **Loht** <term>Loht; a historical unit of weight, roughly half an ounce, here used to measure the brine's density</term> as it comes out of the well, and yields a salt content of thirty *Loht* [of salt] <note>The text describes the high concentration of the brine; 30 parts of salt to 108 parts of liquid suggests a very rich source</note>. The hearths of the pans are bricked up on all sides except for one hole, which is one ell high above the ground, where the fuel is fed in. For this boiling, they use finely split logs. In twenty-four boilings come twelve *Solen* <note>original: "Solen"; here used as a specific production volume</note> as they call them; these are forty-eight pans, which make eight **bulging barrels** <term>Bauchtonnen; large, rounded wooden casks used for transport</term>. Each barrel is worth, on average throughout the year, four **Marks** <term>Marck; a unit of currency</term>, which is equal to two **Thalers** <term>Thaler; a large silver coin used throughout Europe</term>. # Halle in Saxony.

In **Halle in Saxony**, which is said to take its name from the word *Dobreiora* in the **Wendish language** <term>Windischer Sprache; referring to the Slavic Sorbian language spoken in the region</term>—which in English means "Good Mountain"—or, as others would have it, *Dobrefoel*, which means "Good Brine"—there spring forth four distinct salt springs and wells. These are the **German Well**, the **Good Year** <note>original: "Gutjahr"</note>, **Metritz**, and **Hackeborn**. And although they spring up close together, one is richer [in salt content] than the others, just as in many mines located near one another, rich and poor ores are found; particularly the **Metritz**, which is captured barely four paces away from the **German Well**.

The **German Well** is drawn up with **winches** <term>Haspeln; large wooden windlasses used to haul heavy buckets from the depths</term>. They maintain three shifts; every two hours, two well-servants or winchmen take turns, alongside two scoopers who pour out the buckets. There are large buckets on the winches, each holding a **tun** <term>Tonnen; a large cask or barrel measure</term>. The well is twenty **fathoms** <term>Klaffter; a measure of depth, roughly 1.8 meters or 6 feet</term> or sixty **ells** <term>Elen; a unit of length based on the forearm</term> deep. At its opening, it is wide at the top,

The **Metritz** is also deep; it is driven by a **treadwheel** <term>Kefferrad; a large hollow wooden wheel powered by men walking inside it to lift heavy loads</term>. Three shifts are maintained, and two men are always inside the treadwheel; each earns one **Guilder** <term>Gülden; a gold or silver coin used as a standard currency</term> per week.

**Good Year** <note>original: "Gutjahr"</note> the well is the best in quality after the German Well. It can be locked up all around and is operated by a treadwheel. Two men are also in it at any given shift, along with the scooper who pours out the bucket that has been drawn up. They maintain three shifts. It has a muddy bottom and is about 18 **fathoms** <term>Lachter; a mining unit of length, approximately 2 meters or 6.5 feet</term> deep.

**Hackeborn** is also drawn up by a treadwheel, like the previous two; this well is 45 **ells** <term>Elen; a measurement based on the length of a forearm, roughly 0.6 meters</term> deep.

The **salt-houses** <term>Koda; specialized huts or shacks used for boiling brine into salt</term> in Halle are built above ground; they are quite spacious. ### The Third Part

[The hearth is] enclosed at the front and on the sides everywhere. The <term>Pfannenloch</term> <note>The fire-hole or opening where fuel is added to the hearth beneath the pan</note> is one foot wide, built up from the ground with baked bricks. And when one fires with straw, an **iron rod** <term>Eysen</term> two ells long is inserted into the hole at the front, so that the straw does not fall onto the hearth too quickly. For one <term>Werck</term> <note>A technical term for a single production cycle or "batch" of salt</note>, one fills thirty-six buckets, and when it has boiled down so far—approximately two fingers' width—and begins to **plaster** <note>original: "pflastern"; this refers to the salt forming a solid crust or "pavement" on the surface as the water evaporates</note>, then it is time and quite enough. [The workers] pour in a small cheese-bowl full of <term>Schwenckebier</term> <note>Literally "swill-beer" or "rinse-beer"; beer was often used in historical salt-making as a clarifying agent to help impurities rise to the top as foam</note>, place a board at the front of the pan, and let it **sink** <term>socken; the process of the brine draining away as the salt settles and solidifies</term>. This occurs in a quarter of an hour. When it has settled sufficiently, one carries the pieces of the previous batch from the pan to the <term>Saltzstete</term> <note>The "salt-place" or drying area where finished salt was stored</note>. They set up two empty baskets, gather the salt in the pan together, and shovel the salt into the baskets. When the baskets are full, one smooths all around the baskets with the shovel and then strikes with the shovel all around the basket, three strikes one after 
another—

...one another, so that there is as much salt **above the basket** as there is **within the basket**. Two pieces are made at a time, or in one **batch** <term>Werck; a single production cycle</term>. One such piece holds ten and a half <note>original: "eilff-halb", an archaic fraction meaning 10.5</note> **Nordhausen quarters** <note>A dry measure of volume from the nearby town of Nordhausen</note>. They boil in a **boiling hut** <term>Kode; the specialized building where salt was refined</term> throughout the week. For a "full boiling," three **shock** <note>A "Schock" is a unit of sixty; thus 180 tubs</note> of **tub-brine** <term>Zubber-sole</term> are used. Forty batches result in forty **thick Thalers** <note>Large, high-value silver coins</note> in money. One hundred and five boiling huts are operational, in which boiling is carried out continuously.

There are also three "building-huts"; if a **nobleman** <term>Juncker; here referring to the hereditary owners of the salt springs</term> must rebuild his hut, one of these is granted to him in the meantime so that he may still produce his salt during that period and need not lose any work.

One piece of salt is worth 13 good silver **Groschen** and 6 **Pfennigs** in money. One must also have three new **pans** every year. However, barely two are actually purchased, because so much sheet metal remains from them that one can construct the third, which is called the **Cross-pan** <term>Creutzpfan</term>; one pan costs 8 florins and 13 groschen.

### The Third Part

**Fuel** is expensive at one time and cheap at another; accordingly, the **foremen** <term>Vorſchleger; supervisors who set production rates</term> assess the value of the **brine** <term>Sole</term>. A great deal of **split logs** <term>Scheidholtz</term>, which are floated down the **Saale** <note>The river Saale was the primary transport route for timber from the Thuringian forests to the salt-works at Halle</note> and caught there, are also used for the boiling.

The **noblemen** <term>Junckern; the hereditary class of salt-works owners in Halle, known as Pfänner</term> provide the fuel and cover all expenses; therefore, the **worker** <term>Wircker; the skilled laborer who works the salt in the pan</term> or master must provide an account of the fuel, the brine, and the salt to the nobleman every Sunday.

The worker receives one good **Groschen** <term>A common silver coin</term> for every piece; this amounts to 80 Groschen weekly for a full boiling cycle. From this, he gives the laborer 11 Groschen and the **filler** <term>Voll-ſchütter; the worker responsible for pouring the brine</term> 8 Groschen. The laborer receives 4 **Pfennigs** <term>Smallest currency unit</term> for every piece, which the **customer** <note>original: "Gaſt"; literally "guest," referring to the salt purchaser</note> pays. The **ash-handler** <term>Gruder; from "Grude," the worker who manages the embers and ash beneath the pan</term> or "five-groschen laborer" earns one Pfennig for one batch of material poured. Otherwise, six Pfennigs are paid to prepare a batch, and two Pfennigs for the necessary filling. For making a new **hearth** <term>Herd; the stone or brick structure supporting the salt pan</term>, one pays three **shock** <note>A "Schock" is a unit of sixty; here likely referring to 180 Groschen total</note>, and the porter earns one [Pfennig] for carrying each piece up.

...out, in which a little saltpeter <note>original: *Niter*</note> is found. And because a great deal of "wild water" <note>Unwanted groundwater or freshwater that dilutes the salt concentration</note> flows into various shafts <term>Schachten</term>, a tunnel <term>Stollen</term> was driven through a mountain nearly a mile above the town. Through this, water is channeled to drive the wheels of the **pumping engine** <term>Kunst; a complex mechanical system for moving water</term> for the extraction of the wild water as well as the good brine <term>Sole</term>. All of this is raised with chains to which hoses are attached. Because of the great quantity of wild water that must be extracted, this engine is so constructed that its equal is no longer found at any other salt-works in Germany.

The wild water is drawn up through eleven different pipes <term>Röhren</term>, each of which is four and a half inches <term>fünffthalben Zoll</term> wide. This wild water, which itself contains five or six **Lot** <note>A unit of weight; the text indicates that even the "waste" water had a low salt concentration</note> of salt, is raised together with the good brine. When the good brine is drawn up from the depths of the well to above the earth, the brine flows into a large, wide, and deep wooden vat <term>Pottich</term>, where a large and very high wheel, 36 feet <term>schuch; literally "shoes"</term> high—

# The Third Part

...is hung, which is driven by the water channeled onto it from above in gutters. Through this, the good brine <term>Sole</term> is raised even higher through hoses—much like onto a high tower—to a height of 72 cubits <term>Elen</term> <note>An *Elle* or cubit was a traditional unit of measurement; in this region, it was roughly 2 feet or 60 cm</note> from the innermost depths. From there, the salt brine is further divided into gutters and led toward the salt-sheds <term>Sölden</term> <note>Specialized buildings or booths used in the salt production process</note>.

Ladders are placed on hooks within the well, upon which one can climb down and inspect the brine.

The well itself, from which the good brine is drawn, is 36 cubits deep.

There are one hundred and seventeen salt-sheds <term>Sölden</term> or huts <term>Kode</term> to be found there. These lie deep in the earth, built up with masonry walls and roofed with wooden shingles. They are quite wide, long, and spacious so that, when necessary, a good supply of timber <note>original: *gehölke*; wood used as fuel for the boiling process</note> can be stored inside. In each salt-shed are five or six large vats <term>Bötche</term>, some of which are 12 feet <term>Schuhe</term> <note>literally "shoes"</note> wide and 18 feet high. In these, a reserve of brine is kept, so that when—

In wintertime, the machinery <term>Kunst</term> <note>original: *Kunst*; here referring to the mechanical pump system or "water-work" described on the previous page</note> freezes due to great cold and cannot operate, so that the pan-masters <term>Pfannenherrn</term> <note>The owners or master operators of the salt pans</note> nonetheless have enough from the supply to continue boiling, as many can keep a supply sufficient for several weeks.

The hearth and pan are set up in the following manner: they are made with slag <term>Schlotter</term> <note>original: *Schlotter*; in this context, it refers to the stony waste material or sediment from the salt-works used for insulation and masonry</note>, measuring 12 feet <term>Schuhe</term> <note>literally "shoes"</note> long and 11 feet wide in circumference. On the inside, however, there is a half-hearth, raised one and a half feet, built up in front with baked stones <note>meaning bricks</note> and then with slag on both sides. The pans are exactly as long and wide as the pans at Allendorf in Hesse, being twelve feet long, eleven wide, and half a foot deep. However, they are not as heavy because thinner iron plating <term>Eisenblech</term> is used, which reduces the weight. Yet one can last as long as the other, for in each place a pan must last five years. A trench one and a half feet wide runs through the middle of the hearth. Iron bars are placed upon this so that the fuel <term>Feuerwerk</term> <note>original: *Feuerwerck*; referring here to the arrangement of firewood or the fire-grate structure</note> can be laid upon them; these bars, when

One always keeps enough supply <note>of brine</note> in the pan to make four pieces of salt from it. # Of Salt Minerals.

a salt shovel <term>Salzscheufftein</term> <note>A specialized tool, likely a small wooden shovel, used to pat and shape the moist salt</note> and binds it with a bast fiber band <note>original: *pastern Bande*; bands made of tough plant fibers used to hold the salt cone together as it forms</note>, eleven of which are tied around before the piece is finished. Each piece is worth nine good Groschen in money; of this, the lordship receives 27 Pfennige, and the worker <term>Wircker</term> along with the supervisor <term>Pfleger</term> and the wood-binders <term>Holzbinderinnen</term> <note>Women employed specifically to bundle the brushwood used for the fires</note> receive seven and a half <note>original: *achtehalben*; literally "the eighth half"</note> Groschen for every twelve pieces. ### The Third Part

The good brine consistently contains 14 *Lot* <note>original: *Lot*; a traditional unit of weight, usually 1/32 of a pound. This measurement indicates the salt concentration within the liquid brine.</note> per *Centner* <term>hundredweight</term>. The tools used for salt boiling are, first: a *Nord* <note>likely a heavy striking block or anvil-like surface</note> upon which one strikes the pieces; a salt shovel; an ash scoop; and a cleaning hammer <term>Fegehammer</term> with which the salt stone <note>A hard mineral crust or "boiler scale" that builds up on the salt pan during boiling</note> is knocked off, which occurs once every 12, 14, or 15 workdays. Furthermore, a pick <term>Bicken</term> with which one breaks out the brine-conduit <term>Beisse</term> <note>The wooden troughs or channels through which the brine was directed</note>, and bands which are placed around the salt pieces. There is also, thank God, a sufficient supply of brine without lack; it is improved through the brine-conduit, just as in other places. And when the brine "seizes up" in the pan and will not crystallize <term>socken</term> <note>The technical term for salt crystals forming and sinking to the bottom of the boiling pan</note> well, one swirls a little beer into it, as is done in other places.

The Customs Official <term>Zöllner</term> <note>An administrative governor acting on behalf of the local counts</note> holds the government over the entire saltworks on behalf of the Lords Counts, and also collects the customs duty from it which the Pan-Lords <term>Pfannen-Herren</term> <note>The investors or wealthy citizens who owned the individual boiling pans</note> must pay annually. ## Allendorf in Hesse.

In Allendorf on the Werra / in Hesse / a salt spring arises / it carries with it a little alum and saltpeter / it rises out of a mineral gravel <note>original: *Kiß*; likely pyritic gravel or mineral-rich sediment</note> from below out of the depths under a limestone mountain / and the boiling houses lie outside the city across the water / the Werra river, a musket-shot <note>A common historical measurement of distance, roughly 150 to 200 yards</note> distance from the city / there are eighty-six operational huts <term>Kode</term> and pans / and the salt-well is built in the following manner / it is not round

The best salt veins <term>Saltzadern</term> <note>The specific channels or fissures in the rock from which the saline water flows</note> are located next to and all around this stone. The other half, however, is about knee-deep; there stand the pipes and the machinery of the pump-works <term>Pompenwerck</term> which raises the brine <term>Sole</term> <note>Concentrated salt water ready for boiling</note>. There are two staircases upon which one can descend. The threshold of the structure is laid upon the gravel and secured on the outside with clay or loam <term>Lätten</term> <note>Sticky, waterproof clay used as a sealant</note> so that the "wild water" <note>original: *wilde Wasser*; refers to freshwater or surface runoff that would dilute the brine and ruin its quality</note> nearby cannot penetrate it. The machinery <term>Kunst</term> <note>A common historical term for a complex mechanical engine or "work of artifice"</note> drives both water and brine simultaneously. As the pump-levers <term>Schwengel</term> scoop from the well, they draw one after the other. This machinery is maintained by horses, of which there are six; two by two, they rotate at specific times, and each pair of horses has an attendant who directs them.

### Of Salt-Minerals

The boiling houses <term>Kode</term> <note>Small industrial huts or "cotes" dedicated to the evaporation of brine</note> are built in this manner: First, at the front and back, there is a large pillar or roller—whichever you might call it—placed into two small supports. Over these, approximately 32 support beams <term>Hengelbäume</term> <note>Literally "hanging-trees"; these were heavy timber beams from which the massive lead or iron boiling pans were suspended over the furnace</note> are laid across both sides. The gables and walls are constructed of timber on all sides and daubed with clay <term>Leymen</term>. The roof is secured with roof-tiles nailed to laths and covered with a layer of thatch.

In every boiling house, there is an ash chamber <term>Aschenkammer</term> where the master salt-boiler pours his ashes. Over the course of about five years, he can sell these ashes to the glassmakers <term>Glaßmachern</term> for roughly two hundred thalers <note>Potash, derived from wood ash, was a vital and expensive ingredient for glass production, making the furnace waste a valuable byproduct</note>.

The hearth and the boiling-pit <term>Gesode</term> are constructed and made from salt-sediment <term>Schlotter</term> <note>A stony residue, primarily calcium sulfate, that precipitates during the boiling process; it was recycled as a heat-resistant lining for the furnaces</note>. At the top, it is twelve feet <term>schuch</term> <note>A "Schuh" (foot) was a historical unit of length; in this region, it was roughly 28 to 30 centimeters</note> long and eleven feet wide—exactly as large, long, and wide as the boiling pans themselves. At the bottom of the hearth, however, it is no longer than six feet and five feet wide, because it is tapered <note>original: *zugeschmiehet*; meaning the walls angle inward toward the grate at the bottom to concentrate the heat</note> from the top down. At the bottom of the hearth stands a large iron bar or grate <term>Rost</term> of cast iron, placed lengthwise, upon which the wood is thrown for fuel. The pan-opening <term>Pfannenloch</term> is

...a little raw brine <term>Sola</term> <note>original: "Sola," an older spelling of "Sole," meaning salt-water or brine</note> is beaten together or mixed / and put into the pan while boiling / then it produces a large, impure foam in the pan / which is gathered together / and taken out / thus the salt is purified. However, such grease-salt <term>Schmiersalz</term> <note>Salt contaminated with fatty residues or impurities gathered during the initial clarification; it was considered lower grade</note> is not useful for the salting of meat / [at least not] until a boiling <term>Sod</term> <note>A "Sod" refers to a single complete cycle or batch of boiling brine until crystallization</note> has occurred again after the cleaning. When the salt has been fully boiled / then a large, wide cloth is drawn over the entire pan / so that nothing impure can fall into it / for the brine <term>Sole</term> cannot tolerate much [interference] / especially if a little "feather-matter" <term>Federmateria</term> <note>Likely referring to organic contaminants such as down or dust; salt-makers were notoriously protective of the brine's surface tension, as even a small amount of fat or organic material could prevent the salt from "graining" or crystallizing properly</note> gets into it / then it will not turn into salt at all. When it has now stood and settled <term>gesöcket</term> <note>The process of allowing the crystals to sink and "soak" in the remaining hot liquid to grow larger</note> for one / three / or four hours / then twelve / or even sixteen "geese" <term>Gense</term> <note>Technical term for the large, rounded heaps of wet salt scooped out of the pan, named for their white, rounded appearance resembling a sitting bird</note> are caught out with shovels. When these have drained off <note>original: "vbertroffen," referring to the removal of the excess surface brine</note> upon the pan / they are pushed onto the front and rear ovens / and dried next to the pan on the sides. More of these "geese" are then caught out / when salt has formed in the pan once again /

It also details the harvesting of the salt into heaps called "geese" for drying on the oven ledges.</summary>

Coal <term>Steinkohlen</term>, however, is not used for boiling in more than three

for one pan; each measure costs

# The Third Part

...content <note>Referring to the salt concentration in the brine</note>, which many cannot wrap their heads around; but it is commonly found to be six *Lot* <note>A unit of weight; in this context, it likely indicates the amount of salt yield per unit of brine, approximately 15-16 grams</note>.

The Landgrave <term>Landgraf</term> <note>A nobleman of princely rank with jurisdiction over a territory</note> oversees the entire saltworks and gives the pan-lords <term>Pfannen-Herren</term> <note>The owners or shareholders of the salt-boiling pans</note> a monthly share of 700 Guilders <term>Gülden</term>, 7 Albus, and 7 Heller. An entire pan-share or boiling works <term>Siedewerck</term> costs 3,200 Guilders.

The Salt Count <term>Salzgraf</term> <note>The chief administrator or judge of the saltworks</note> governs the entire works; the Treasurer <term>Rentmeister</term> collects the money from the boiling process; the Wood Bailiff <term>Holzvoigt</term> and the Faggot-scribe <term>Wellenschreiber</term> <note>An official responsible for tracking bundles of brushwood used for fuel</note> manage the wood and coal money. The Village Mayor <term>Schultheis</term> helps manage the wood-floating, as most of the timber is brought to the site by water on rafts and ships. The Customs Officer or the Treasurer’s counter-scribe collects the tax on the salt: 2 Pfennigs for every eighth-part, and one Heller for every Malter <note>A dry measure of volume</note> of ash.

These officials meet every Monday and close the weekly accounts. If any further defects or problems have occurred in the saltworks, they attend to them immediately. Otherwise, there is also held a

It is also currently planned and underway that a new straw graduation tower <term>Strohleckwerck</term> <note>A structure used to concentrate brine through natural evaporation by trickling it over bundles of straw or brushwood</note> is being established there; may God grant His blessing to it. And let this be sufficient regarding this saltworks.

### The Third Part

The water is drawn out with a capstan wheel <term>Keffer rad</term> <note>A large geared wheel or treadwheel used as a winch to lift heavy loads</note>. A building has been constructed over the well-spring, and from this spring, the brine is guided through pipes into the town and the saltworks <term>Saltzwerck</term>, into another enclosed basin. There, the brine <term>Sole</term> is drawn up with two buckets, which are raised by two lever-arms <term>Schwengeln</term> <note>Also known as a "swipe" or "shadoof," these are counterweighted poles used to lift buckets from a well</note>, and then poured into buckets so that it can flow into the collection vats <term>Kode</term>. There are twelve of these vats—or "naps" <term>Nappen</term>, as they call them—at that location. These troughs are built into the ground, with a straw roof placed over them. The hearth is also about twelve feet <term>Schuch</term> in circumference and is closed on all sides, except for the front which is wide open where the wood is inserted. Likewise, an opening is made at the back so that the flames <term>Lohe</term> and steam can escape. They pack the hearth so full of logs, tree trunks, and coarse timber that a powerful heat is generated beneath, for the iron of the pan <term>Pfanneysen</term> is also quite thick there. The pan is perfectly square, ten and a half <note>original: "eilfftehalbend," an archaic fractional counting method meaning 'the eleventh half,' or 10.5</note> feet long and wide, and half a foot deep. The hearth and the boiling-structure <term>Gesode</term> are made from sediment-stone <term>Schlotter</term> <note>The mineral scale or crust—mostly calcium and magnesium—that precipitates during the boiling of brine; it was often used as a heat-resistant building material for salt-furnaces</note>.

# Of Salt Minerals.

Behind the pans, they also have a large **brine-cistern** <term>Sohlstock</term> <note>A wooden reservoir used to store or settle brine during the boiling process</note> in which they steep <note>original: "beisset," referring to the settling or "biting" of the brine</note> the liquid. Next to it, they also have a **sediment-box** <term>Schlotterkasten</term> in which they collect the dross <term>Schlotter</term> <note>The "schlotter" or "shlot" refers to the calcium and magnesium sulfates that precipitate early in the boiling process before the sodium chloride crystallizes</note>. One pulls out a plug when one wishes to clean the brine-cistern, so that the sediment flows out.

They boil it until it is quite dry, then set up two baskets made of boards. These are narrow at the bottom and become wider toward the top, just like a **lye-strainer** <term>Laugensiegen</term> <note>A conical basket used for filtering liquid through ash to produce lye</note> or the hopper <term>Rumpff</term> in a mill where the grain is poured for grinding. They are constructed with two long handles <term>Steuren</term> on each side, by which they are lifted.

These baskets are emptied <note>original: "außgeschlagen," literally "knocked out"</note> with an iron shovel. Six of these salt pieces <note>large molded cakes or blocks of salt</note> are produced from one pan in 24 hours, which is called a **quarter** <term>Viertel</term>. In one week, four quarters are made, totaling 24 pieces; this is the amount of salt boiled in one **boiling-hut** <term>Kode</term> <note>A "salt-cot" or small building housing the boiling operations</note> per week. The **salt-lord** <term>Pfannherr</term> <note>The owner of the salt pan or the legal right to boil salt</note> sells the salt, and pays the two laborers and...

# The Third Part

The workers are each paid **six groschen** <note>A silver currency used in German lands</note> for one **quarter** <term>Viertel</term>; they must perform both the salt-making and the stirring together. The tools used here include **salt-baskets**, an iron **salt-shovel**, a **pick** <term>Bicken</term> with which the **crust** <term>Beisse</term> <note>The hard mineral scale or "bitterns" that builds up on the pan during boiling</note> is broken out, a large **brine-cistern** <term>Sohlstock</term>, an iron **fork**, an **ash-shovel** <term>Aschenschüpfsen</term>, a **scraping iron** <term>Schrapeysen</term>, and a **sweeping hammer** <term>Fegehammer</term>. This constitutes the equipment used there. The pan is also cleaned and swept at the proper time. There is a large supply of **crust** there, as the works and quarters are contracted out. Therefore, they "improve" the **boilings** <term>Gesöde</term> with the brine, which results in a loss for the **contractors** <term>Dinggästen</term> <note>Parties who have contracted for a specific yield or quantity</note> but is to the advantage of the **salt-lords** <term>Pfannern</term> and the **salt-workers**.

In this place, the salt-lords have the saltworks in their hands and manage everything, except that they have among them two **overseers** and **salt-counts** <term>Saltzgrafen</term> <note>High-ranking officials or noblemen appointed to supervise salt production and taxation</note>, who are appointed to ensure the profit and advancement of the saltworks at all times.
