# Lüder

**Lüder** <note>modern Lüder, a district in Großenlüder</note>, located in the **Abbey of Fulda** <note>An influential ecclesiastical territory and Benedictine monastery in present-day Hesse</note>; a salt spring rises there. In former times, well-meaning people began to develop it in the hope of making it profitable. Afterward, the work was carried further by people from **Augsburg** and **Nuremberg**, but it achieved no permanence until recently. A few years ago, through the advice and proposals of salt-making experts, it was brought into steady operation. The **brine** <term>Sole</term> is drawn up with a **windlass** <term>Haspel</term> that has a large bucket attached to a chain. It is a **salt-hut** <term>Koth</term> <note>original: "Koth"; a specialized small-scale boiling house or facility for salt production</note> from which a property owner can derive a reasonably good annual profit. The pan is nine feet wide and ten feet long, and where wood is scarce, **heather** <term>Heyden</term> is also used there for boiling. In a day and a night, one pan of salt is produced and yielded, and the salt there is of quite high value. The raw brine from

# Orb.

<note>modern Bad Orb</note>

**T**his saltworks is located six miles from **Lüder** / there the main salt spring is situated outside the city / which is driven by the "wild water" <note>Natural stream water used to power machinery</note> / which falls onto a wheel and draws up the **brine** <term>Sole</term> with hoses / it is conducted into the city into another well through wooden channels / which run high above the ground / at which well there is also a **water-engine** <term>Wasserkunst</term> <note>A complex mechanical pumping system powered by a water wheel</note> and they separate the brines there / this engine drives the brine / so that it flows into all the **salt-huts** <term>Kote</term> <note>Small-scale boiling houses for salt production</note> /

The **boiling houses** <term>Kohte</term> <note>Small buildings used specifically for boiling brine to extract salt</note> are arranged in the following manner: the **gables** <term>Gübbel</term> at the front and back are made entirely of stone, and on both sides above, there is a **thatched roof** <term>Strodach</term>. At this end, there is also a large **brine reservoir** <term>Solstock</term> <note>A large wooden cistern or container for storing the salt water</note>, which can easily last for a man's lifetime. The hearths are narrow at the bottom, filled with **sediment** <term>Schlotter</term> <note>The waste residue or "mud" that precipitates during the boiling of the brine</note>, and wide at the top. The **boiling pans** <term>Pfannen</term> set upon them are eleven and a half **feet** <term>Schuh</term> <note>original: "Schuh"; literally "shoes," a historical unit of measurement roughly equivalent to a foot</note> long and wide, and are perfectly square. In each boiling house, they boil two **shifts** <term>Wercke</term> <note>A specific production cycle or set amount of work</note> per week, from which salt worth eighteen **guilders** <term>gülden</term> is produced. They begin boiling on Sunday and continue until Thursday; they refill the brine ten times and finish the process over the coals in a single day.

Regarding how it is prepared: they have large round hearths; they place the salt on the **pan-beams** <term>Pfannenbeume</term> <note>Supporting timber structures used in the salt-drying process</note> and strike it with a shovel; there is a square base at the bottom, and they fill it in the middle; this is done repeatedly until at last it is completely finished. # The Third Part

as a man, three and a half **cubits** <term>Ellen</term> <note>original: "vierdhalb Ellen"; an "Ell" was a historical unit of measurement roughly 24 inches, so this refers to a height of about seven feet</note> high; at the bottom it is equally square, but somewhat larger; it stands like a hewn stone; five of these are made from one **pan** <term>Pfannen</term>.

Regarding worker wages: the master earns one **guilder** <term>Gülden</term> per week; likewise, the patron <note>original: "Gast"; here referring to the owner or employer</note> provides him with a meal every time he is invited. To the servant <note>apprentice or laborer</note>, they give 8 **groschen** <term>Groschen</term> per week, and his **gratuity** <term>Tranckgeld</term> <note>literally "drink money," a standard bonus or allowance for laborers</note> amounts to twelve groschen for a week's earnings, not including his firewood and other such provisions. The instruments they use are: the **salt-scoop** <term>Segesaß</term>, **brine-vats** <term>Solfasse</term> in which one **treats** <note>original: "beißt"; refers to a specific step in the crystallization or refining process</note> the salt, and an iron **pick** <term>Bicken</term> with which one handles the treatment pans. The **saltworks** <term>Saltzwerck</term> are owned by the citizens and **salt-proprietors** <term>Pfenner</term> <note>Wealthy individuals who held hereditary rights to boil salt in specific pans</note> of that place; however, they also have their own administrators and **salt-counts** <term>Salzgrafen</term> <note>High-ranking officials responsible for the legal and administrative oversight of the salt industry</note> among them.

In one **boiling house** <term>Kohte</term>, fifteen guilders' worth of **fuel** <term>Feuerwerck</term> <note>original: "Feuerwerck"; here meaning wood or combustible material for the hearths, rather than modern fireworks</note> is used each week. The ownership of the pans is also divided, such that three or four people share one boiling house. This saltworks is situated under the jurisdiction of the **Bishop of Mainz** <note>The Prince-Archbishop and Elector of Mainz, who held secular and religious authority over the region</note>.

The brine there is also weak, holding barely four, or sometimes five, **lots** <term>Loth</term> <note>a historical unit of weight; a "Loth" was approximately 1/32nd of a pound, indicating a very low salt concentration here</note>.

## Nauheim

Nauheim <note>modern-day Bad Nauheim</note> lies three **miles** <term>Meilen</term> <note>historical German miles were significantly longer than modern English miles, approximately 4.6 miles or 7.5 kilometers each</note> from Frankfurt am Main; there they have no enclosed salt-well, but instead several swamps that are fenced all around. From these swamps run several ditches and channels, from which the brine is scooped and subsequently flows into the **boiling house** <term>Kohte</term>. The boiling houses there are thatched with straw; the walls are made of wickerwork and daubed with mud. Furthermore, the hearths there are made of **sediment** <term>Schlotter</term> <note>original: "Schlotter"; the stony residue or "scale" that precipitates out of the brine during boiling, which was often recycled as a heat-resistant material for furnace construction</note>. The hearth begins at a hole on the ground a **foot** <term>Schuh</term> wide and grows wider as it rises toward the pan, where the wood and fuel are thrown in. The pans there are nearly six feet long and wide; the salt is packed into baskets. The **brine-treatment** <term>Beisse</term> <note>a refining process involving additives to clarify the salt</note> is also used in this place, but the salt is not purified; rather, it is made quite gray. Since, however

the peasants there boil it themselves, and because they never make more than they need from one time to the next, one can have no certainty regarding how much salt is made in a week or how much wood is consumed. Likewise, one cannot know the workers' wages for certain, as everything is inconsistent. The **tools** <term>Instrumenta</term> <note>original Latin: "Instrumenta"</note> they use are **salt baskets** <term>Salzkörbe</term>, **salt scoops** <term>Salzschupffen</term>, **scraping hammers** <term>Fegehammer</term> <note>used to remove the crust or scale from the boiling pans</note>, **picks** <term>Bicken</term>, and an iron shovel. Furthermore, there are no formal **salt regulations** <term>Salzordnung</term> or established customs, nor are there any actual **salt-workers** <term>Salzknechte</term> <note>professional laborers trained specifically in salt production</note>; rather, the peasants, as mentioned, only boil as much as they need at any given time and whenever it is necessary for them.

There are ten **boiling houses** <term>Kohte</term> there; the **brine** <term>Sole</term> is also quite weak. Otherwise, there is a sufficient supply of timber in that area. The work could also be arranged much more efficiently—so that fuel would be saved and the salt made fine, clean, and **coarse-grained** <term>grobkörnig</term>—if one employed people there who had experience in such trades and arts. This is as much as is necessary to report regarding this **salt-works** <term>Salzwerck</term>.

## Hall in Swabia.

**Hall in Swabia** <note>Though the text places this in Swabia, the reference to Innsbruck confirms this is Hall in Tirol, a major salt production center since the 13th century.</note> is located one mile from **Innsbruck** <note>original: "Ißbruck"</note>; there are many **boiling houses** <term>Kohte</term> <note>specialized huts where brine was evaporated</note> there. It has large **pans** <term>Pfannen</term> which hang on chains; the hearth is enclosed all around, except for the front where the fire is stoked. When a pan has finished boiling, four men step up to iron bars prepared for this purpose and use large iron shovels to scoop the salt out. A great deal of salt is produced there. There is also a large body of water there, upon which the timber and firewood are floated down to the salt-works as whole trees; on every raft, each **nobleman** <term>Juncker</term> <note>a member of the landed nobility or a young lord</note> has his own mark so that no fraud may be committed.

The citizens and **pan-lords** <term>Pfannen Herrn</term> <note>the owners or shareholders of the individual boiling pans</note> manage the salt-works; however, they have several superiors and administrators among them who govern these matters in an orderly fashion. The **salt-well** <term>Saltzbrunn</term> is also constructed in a circular shape, nearly thirty **fathoms** <term>Claffter</term> <note>a historical unit of depth, roughly 6 feet or 1.8 meters</note> deep. The brine is drawn up with buckets and guided through channels into all the boiling houses.

# Aueleben.

**At Aueleben** <note>likely modern-day Auleben, located near the Kyffhäuser hills</note>, the **brine** <term>Sola</term> shifts from one place to another / so that they cannot achieve a thorough containment / it has moved into an adjacent shaft / so that the hole / where it had previously stood / is quite dry / a pipe has been installed there / and the **wild water** <note>fresh water used to provide mechanical power, rather than salt water for boiling</note> / which is guided from **Heringen** <note>Heringen/Helme</note> a considerable mile away / has been directed onto a wheel / falling beside it / which brought the brine out through a pipe and hoses / beside and above there were two water-hoses / which were also driven out by the same water / on the other side there are also three hoses / which were brought up through the wild water with **gear-work** <term>Hengen</term> <note>referring to the mechanical linkages or suspension systems used to transmit power</note> / and such shafts are about 20 **fathoms** <term>Clafftern</term> <note>a unit of depth, roughly 6 feet or 1.8 meters</note> deep / but at first there were two **horse-mills** <term>Roßkünste</term> <note>large horse-driven machines used for pumping or hoisting</note> / upon which 18 horses were once kept / six for each **shift** <term>Schicht</term> / but they did not hold the water sufficiently. In **Artern**, in the **Lordship of Mansfeld** / there is a salt spring / located approximately a **musket shot** <note>a historical unit of distance, roughly 150 to 250 yards</note> from the town near the **Lime Valley** <term>Kalckthal</term> / which **Elector Augustus of Saxony** / of most honorable memory / brought into a **masonry conduit** <term>Rörckmaure</term> / and newly with 22 anse- <note>original: "anse-", likely the start of "ansehnlichen" or "respectable/substantial"</note>

->**of Salt Minerals.**<-

...substantial boiling houses <term>Sölden</term> <note>small huts or buildings where brine was boiled down into salt</note> built; the well was framed in a square with fir planks, and an engine house <term>Kunsthauß</term> <note>a building housing the technical machinery, such as pumps or waterwheels</note> was built over it. The surface water <term>wilde Wasser</term> <note>non-saline water, such as groundwater or runoff, used here to drive the machinery</note> drove a wheel, by which the brine <term>Sole</term> was lifted into the channel <term>Gerinne</term> with pumps, and led further through channels from one boiling house to the others.

Many methods were indeed attempted to remove the surface water from the brine and to improve its quality. To this end, several small pans were placed behind the large pan, each higher than the next in a stepped fashion. There, the brine was warmed in these smaller pans and flowed from one into the other, and finally into the large pan. Through this method, it was believed that the surface water would evaporate <note>literally "smoke away"</note> to some extent.

Special lead piping was also made for this purpose by a master tinsmith <term>Kandelgiesser</term> <note>a craftsman specializing in lead and tin conduits and vessels</note> (who was kept constantly in the Salt Valley <term>Saltzthal</term> with a journeyman), and a great deal of expense was spent on it.

# The Third Part

Because of the local shortage, the wood—consisting of brushwood <term>Wellholtz</term> from the Harz and large split logs <term>Scheitholtz</term> from the Elector of Saxony’s territory—was transported there in great quantities by His Electoral Grace’s <note>referring to Elector Augustus of Saxony</note> subjects. Joachim Schreypogel was retained as the inspector or administrator, who had oversight of all matters, and everything was arranged in an orderly and efficient manner. The hearth was constructed of sediment <term>Schlotter</term> <note>original: "Schlotter"; a hard mineral scale, mostly calcium sulfate, that forms on the pans and was reused here as a heat-resistant lining for the furnace</note>, and the additive <term>Beiße</term> <note>a clearing agent used to precipitate impurities from the brine</note> was used just as it is in Artern <note>original: "Arneleben"</note>. Often, more than 24 "works" <term>Wercke</term> <note>a specific unit of production volume</note> were boiled off weekly, amounting to 48 pieces; one piece cost 8 Groschen and held seven and a half measures <term>Maß</term>. The pans were 5 feet <term>Schuch</term> long and 4 wide, and had 5 pan hooks. The master earned 6 Pfennige from each piece, and 6 Groschen for stoking <term>unterzustoßen</term>.

The tools used there were salt baskets, a salt shovel, a cleaning barrel <term>Fegefaß</term>, a cleaning hammer, a fire hook, a pick, a coal rake, and an iron fork.

-># Of Salt Minerals.<-

The raw brine <term>Sole</term> <note>a natural saltwater solution</note> weighs 98 loth and 2 quentchen per quart <note>original: "loht" and "quent"; historical units of weight where a loth was about 14.5 grams and a quentchen was a quarter of a loth</note>, and contains eight loth minus one quentchen of salt. The brine flows out so strongly and powerfully that it could turn a mill wheel. And although this salt-work was maintained for a time in Swabia <note>original: "Schwaben"; though the neighboring towns are in Thuringia, the text specifies the Swabian region here</note>, it eventually fell into complete decline because of a lack of fuel <term>Fewrwercks</term> <note>primarily firewood needed to boil the brine</note> and because it could not cover its expenses or produce a profit. It was sold as a hereditary possession to the Imperial Counts of Schwarzburg—the neighbors of the Frankenhausen salt-works—for a specific and significant sum of money.

# Sulza.

Previously, a master engineer <term>Kunstmeister</term> <note>a specialist in mechanical arts, often responsible for the pumps and technical infrastructure of a mine</note> together with the bailiff <term>Voigt</term> of Sulza undertook to improve the brine there and to elevate the status of the salt-works; to that end, they also constructed two boiling houses <term>Kohte</term> <note>small buildings or huts specifically designed for the evaporation of brine</note>.

## Aschersleben.

At Aschersleben, the well is deep, and the wild water <term>wildes Wasser</term> <note>fresh groundwater that can dilute or contaminate the brine</note> stood about five fathoms <term>Lachter</term> <note>a mining fathom, approximately 2 meters</note> above the brine. Just as the brines are reinforced there below, they are led through the wild water in an inserted pipe and drawn out through hoses. Now, however, a master from Braunschweig <note>Brunswick</note> has constructed a pump-work <term>Pompenwerck</term> in the well, over which a house has been placed. There are three boiling houses <term>Kode</term> <note>small buildings used for boiling the brine to extract salt</note> in operation, which were arranged as follows: they had a half-hearth like those in Frankenhausen, constructed with a sediment-pan <term>Schlotter</term> <note>original: "Schlotter"; a device or basin used to collect the "mud" or initial precipitates during the first stage of brine boiling</note>, and behind it a

## Gross-Salze

At Gross-Salze, there are two salt-wells, just like at Stassfurt, which lies four miles away. One salt-well is operated with a swape-crane <term>Keffer</term> <note>A counterbalanced lever used to lift buckets from a well</note>, the other with a horse-mill <term>Rosskunst</term> and with leather skins <term>Schleuchen</term> <note>Heavy-duty leather bags used for hoisting water or brine from deep shafts</note>. The brine <term>Sole</term> is conducted through pipes and gutters into the boiling-huts <term>Kode</term>. They boil 60 batches <term>Wercke</term> a week in each hut. Each batch consists of two blocks; they do everything just as in Stassfurt.

## Gross-Salze

At Gross-Salze <note>modern-day Schönebeck (Elbe), a historic center of the German salt industry</note>, there are two salt wells, just as in Staßfurt, which lies four miles away. One salt well is brought up with a <term>treadwheel</term> <note>original: "Keffer"; a large wheel-driven crane powered by human or animal labor</note>, and the other with a <term>horse-engine</term> <note>original: "Roßkunst"</note> and with <term>buckets</term> <note>original: "Schleuchen"; literally "tubes" or "hoses," but here referring to the leather bags or containers used to lift brine</note>. The brine is conducted through pipes and channels into the boiling-huts; they boil 60 cycles a week in each hut; each cycle yields two pieces; they do everything just as they do in Staßfurt.

# Kolberg.

In **Kolberg** <note>modern-day Kołobrzeg, Poland</note> in **Farther Pomerania** <note>original: "hinder Pommern"</note> / there are 21 boiling-huts. <meta>This page continues the technical description of the saltworks in Sulza, focusing on the specific fuel used for the fires, the seasonal timeline for production, and the economic arrangements between the master, the laborers, and the local lords.</meta>

->The Third Part<-

Instead of **firewood** <note>original: "Fewerwerg"; here referring to combustible fuel in general</note>, **peat** <note>original: "Dorff"</note> is used there and thrown onto the bricked hearth. When it glows, it falls through [the grate] onto the hearth [floor]; it is then pulled out with a **rake** <note>original: "Krücken"</note> and carried out in front of the **boiling-hut** <note>original: "Kod"</note>.

The peat must be cut in the spring and left to dry until **St. James's Day** <note>July 25th; a traditional marker for the transition from midsummer to the harvest season</note>. Then one boils salt for one to three months, depending on how much fuel one has. One can produce twelve **Thalers** <note>a standard silver coin of the era</note> worth of salt per week in each boiling-hut.

The master must provide the fuel and pay for the pan; he also pays the lords two **Guilders** <note>original: "Gülden"</note> weekly for the right to boil for as long as he operates. The master keeps the remainder, from which he pays the **servants** <note>original: "Gesinde"</note>. A **laborer** <note>original: "Knecht"</note> can earn one Guilder per week. The salt is simply raked out of the pans with a **salt-rake** <note>original: "Salzkrücken"</note> onto the **drying-benches** <note>original: "gesöde"</note>; there it dries, and then it is carried to the **salt-storage** areas <note>original: "Salzstedte"</note> behind the pans.

The following **instruments** <note>original: "Instrumenta"</note> are used here: **Scrapers** <note>original: "Fegesasse"; tools used to clean the residue from the pans</note>, salt-shovels,

of Salt-Minerals.

**Cleaning hammers**, **pan-hooks**, **scrapers**, and **salt-rakes** are used to maintain the hearths and the **boiling-benches** <note>original: "Gesöde"; the raised areas or frames where salt was placed to drain and dry</note>, from which they scrape the crust <note>original: "beissen"; literally 'to bite,' referring to the mechanical action of tools against the mineral buildup or "schlot" in the pans</note>. The price of salt also fluctuates, being worth more at one time than another. Nothing but **peat** <note>original: "Dorff"</note> is used for the boiling.

## Salzgitter.

At Salzgitter <note>original: "Saltzgittel"</note> there are 20 **boiling-huts** <note>original: "Kode"</note>. The hearth is set up directly at the front and is filled in on both sides. In the middle, over the **pit** <note>original: "Grabe"</note>, lie iron bars upon which the wood is thrown. The pan is 6 feet <note>original: "schuch"</note> wide, 7 feet long, and one foot deep; the salt is packed into large baskets. The master earns 1 **Thaler** <note>a large silver coin</note> per week. The **brine** <note>original: "Sohla"</note> there is quite weak, containing five and a half **lots** <note>original: "sechsthalb loht"; a measure of salinity where a 'lot' is approximately 1/32nd of the weight</note>. The scraping-process <note>original: "Beisse"</note> is also used there. Each boiling-hut produces 10 **Guilders** <note>original: "fl"; Florins</note> worth of salt per week. A large iron shovel is used to scoop the salt out into the baskets.

The **instruments** <note>original: "Instrumenta"</note> are: **fire-hooks**, **shovels**, **scrapers**, and **cleaning hammers**. The pan is cleaned every 14 days.

The Third Part

# Salzhemmendorf.

In Salzhemmendorf <note>original: "Saltzhemmendorff"</note> there are 12 **boiling-huts** <note>original: "Kode"; small buildings where brine was evaporated in pans over a fire</note>; the salt there is prepared in every respect as mentioned above.

## Salzthal,

There are two boiling-huts; the salt is also put into baskets there. The pans and hearths are also arranged as described before. The **brine** <note>original: "Sole"</note> has a strength of 6 **lots** <note>original: "Loht"; a measure of salinity where a 'lot' represents roughly 1/32nd of the weight in salt</note>; otherwise, it requires all the same things as stated above.

## Salzdetfurth.

Salzdetfurth <note>original: "Saltzdipper"</note> lies one mile's journey from Hildesheim and has four salt wells; however, one is better and stronger than the others. The water is drawn out using **swape-levers** <note>original: "Schwengeln"; a counterbalanced pole or shadoof used to lift water from a well</note>. There are 32 boiling-huts in that place, but they do not boil during the wintertime. These huts are built directly upon the ground; at the front, the door leads into the middle of the hut. The hearth is filled in on both sides, and at the front there is a wide hole,

<meta>catchword: where (da)</meta>

<summary>This page details the salt production facilities in the towns of Salzhemmendorf, Salzthal, and Salzdetfurth, focusing on the number of boiling-huts and the mechanisms used to extract brine from wells.</summary>
<keywords>Salzhemmendorf, Salzthal, Salzdetfurth, Hildesheim, boiling-huts, brine strength, salt wells, swape-levers, salt production</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the salt-making process in Salzdetfurth, specifically the technical dimensions of the boiling pans, the duties of the sworn measurer, and the economic value and recycling of the salt produced.</meta>

->of Salt Minerals.<-

where the wood is inserted. The pan is eight feet <note>original: "schuch"; literally "shoes," a historical unit of length</note> long and seven wide; it is often refilled, boils for 24 hours, and has two large baskets. These are placed upon the pan and are like chaff-baskets; they are packed full of salt, so that there is as much salt piled on top of the basket as there is inside it. It also has a sworn measurer <term>Messer; an official appointed to ensure honest measurements of the produced salt</term>. When the salt is measured out, the measurer takes 4 measures from each basket; each one of these contains 11 Nordhausen quarters <note>original: "Northeuser Vierteil"; a regional volumetric measure</note>. These four measures cost 7 Gulden, with each Gulden valued at twenty Mariengroschen and 7 Groschen. And when these four measures have been measured out, there remains in the basket about as much as was taken out. Over this, the measurer strews sand <note>likely "Schlot" or "Pfannstein," the mineral scale or sediment that precipitates during the boiling process</note> and then pours it back into the brine reservoir <note>original: "Solstock"</note> as a reserve, so that they may improve the brine <note>original: "Solen"</note> which is otherwise quite weak and meager. However, there is little progress; there is a fair amount of wood nearby, and the Junkers <term>Junckern; the local landed nobility</term> produce the salt.

One of the pans there can last for ten years; they are cleaned <note>original: "fegen"; literally "swept," referring to the removal of mineral scale from the bottom of the pan</note> only once every five weeks. Five Gulden worth of wood is burned.

### The Third Part

Tools <note>original: "Instrumenta"</note> used are: a salt basket, a shovel, fire hooks, iron forks, and a scraper <note>original: "Krücken"; a tool used for raking or scraping the salt from the pan</note>.

A worker earns one Gulden <term>Gulden; a gold or silver coin used throughout the Holy Roman Empire</term> for a week’s labor. The brine <term>Sola; the saltwater solution from which salt is extracted</term> contains 7 *lot* <note>original: "loht"; a historical weight roughly equivalent to 1/32 of a pound or about 15 grams</note> of salt per quart.

# Salt at Helle.

It is located one mile from Einbeck. There are fifteen salt houses <term>Kode; a term for the small huts or boiling houses used in salt production</term> there, but only thirteen are currently operational. They also pack the salt into baskets and require ten Gulden worth of firewood <note>original: "Fewerwerck"</note> for the process, as the brine there is very meager and weak. A quart of raw brine contains twenty-four *lot* <note>likely referring to the total mineral content</note>, and five and a half <note>original: "sechsthalb"; an archaic German counting form meaning 'the sixth is half'</note> *lot* of salt. Each salt house also has a brine reservoir <note>original: "Solstock"; a wooden cistern or container for storing the brine</note> which has lasted beyond human memory. One salt house produces thirteen Gulden worth of salt per week, and in all other respects regarding equipment <note>original: "Gezeug"</note> and usage, it is maintained in the same manner as the aforementioned salt works.

<summary>This page lists the essential tools for salt production and describes the salt works at Helle near Einbeck, noting its operational scale and the relatively low salt concentration of its brine.</summary>

of Salt Minerals.

# Bodenfelde.

This saltworks is located on the Werra river. It consists of nothing more than a single salt house <term>Kod; a small hut or boiling house used for salt production</term> where salt is made. The salt is prepared there in exactly the same way as at the Aldendorf saltworks. The salt well is contained within a reservoir <term>Stock; a wooden cistern or container for storing brine</term>, and the brine <term>Sole; the saltwater solution</term> itself is very weak, containing barely four *lot* <note>original: "loht"; a historical weight roughly equivalent to 1/32 of a pound or about 15 grams per quart</note>. It is, however, sharpened and enriched with the addition of bay salt <note>original: "Boysaltzes"; coarse sea salt, typically imported from the Atlantic coast, added to weak local brine to increase its concentration for boiling</note>. Consequently, in the same time and with the same amount of fuel <note>original: "fewrwerck"</note> required to boil down a pan at Aldendorf, they only manage to produce half as much here. The purchase of wood is inexpensive. The master pays the lords one gold guilder <term>Goldgülden; a high-value gold coin used in the Holy Roman Empire</term> every week.

At Winningen, which is situated under the Duke of Brunswick, there is almost the same kind of brine; a vein of it also springs forth

## Salzuflen.

Located in the Lordship of Lippe; here they use lead pans, just as they do in the style of Lüneburg <note>Lüneburg was the most famous salt-producing city in Northern Germany, known for its extensive use of lead pans for boiling brine</note>. The brine <term>Sole; the saltwater solution</term>, however, is weaker <note>meaning it has a lower salt concentration than other springs</note>; they boil it until it is completely dry in the pans, then rake it out. A great deal of salt is produced here, and there is also a fair amount of woodland surrounding it <note>wood was essential as fuel for the boiling process</note>. A master can earn one and a half guilders <term>Gülden; a gold or silver coin used throughout the Holy Roman Empire</term> a week; a servant earns one guilder.

## At Dreckode.

In this place <note>the text refers to "Zun Dreckoden," modern-day Bad Essen-Dreckode</note>, they also use the same methods as mentioned above, regarding the tools, wages, and the sale of salt. It also has its own measures with which the salt is measured, as well as specific administrative officials <term>Amptsverwalter; bailiffs or regional managers</term> who oversee and govern it. There is also much wood located in the surrounding area.

# Sülldorf.

In the Diocese of Magdeburg lies another salt works at Sülldorf <note>today Sülldorf, part of the Sülzetal municipality, known for its salt marshes</note>, and salt is boiled <note>the process of heating brine in large pans to crystallize the salt</note> there with profit and surplus. There are also other salt veins here and there in the Prince-Archbishopric of Magdeburg, though they remain unworked, such as those under the Junkers <note>noble landowners</note> of Trotha, those of the Asseburg family, and in other places as well. Indeed, the Prince-Archbishopric of Magdeburg is particularly and richly endowed with magnificent salt springs beyond other regions.

## Creuzburg on the Werra.

In Creuzburg, a town on the Werra river belonging to the Dukes of Saxony, a salt spring is found. Some years ago, a graduation works <term>Leckwerck; an early technical structure where brine trickled over bundles of straw or brushwood to evaporate water and increase salt concentration</term> was also started there using straw. However, because the "wild water" <note>unwanted freshwater from rain or springs that dilutes the brine</note> from the lake forcefully overwhelmed the brine <term>Sole; the saltwater solution</term>, the project fell apart and was abandoned.

# Neustadt.

AT Neustadt near the Harzburg <note>today Bad Harzburg</note>, located in the Harz mountains, a vein of salt is found; however, it has not yet been managed with the best profit or brought to its proper state. Similarly, a salt vein is detected at Hundeburg on the Lüneburg Heath. Furthermore, near Veltheim in the Duchy of Brunswick, a fairly good salt brine <term>Saltzsohle; a natural saline solution or brine</term> is found, which carries with it niter <note>saltpeter</note> and alum <note>a mineral salt used in tanning and dyeing</note>.

# Kissingen in Franconia.

There is a salt spring there <note>Bad Kissingen, one of Germany's most famous spa towns</note> which is first enriched by means of a straw graduation works <term>Strohleckwerck; a structure where brine trickles over straw to evaporate water and increase the salt concentration</term>; it is of low yield. The brine brings with it niter, alum, and sulfur.

## Diedelkopf.

Diedelkopf <note>today part of the town of Kusel</note>, in the Duchy of Zweibrücken, near the city of Kusel, in the district of Lichtenberg, has two salt veins found there; they contain vitriol and alum <note>a mineral salt used as a mordant in dyeing</note> alongside the salt. One spring holds two <insert>Loth</insert>, the other holds one Loth <term>Loth; a historical German unit of weight, approximately 14 to 17 grams or half an ounce</term>.

## Bevergern.

Bevergern <note>original: "Biebergeil," a historical name for this town in Westphalia</note>, five miles behind the city of Münster in Westphalia, belonging to the Bishop, is a salt vein; it holds two pounds <note>likely referring to the salt content per measurement of brine</note> and contains sulfur and an arsenical substance, though mixed with a little niter <term>Salniter; also known as saltpeter, used in making gunpowder</term>.

## Schöningen.

Schöningen, in the Duchy of Braunschweig, contains a spring of salt; it carries with it niter <term>Niter; also known as saltpeter, used in making gunpowder and as a preservative</term>, alum, and a little sulfurous matter; otherwise, good salt can be boiled from this brine.

## Kronach.

Not far from the city of Kronach <note>original: "Cranach"</note>, under the Bishop of Bamberg, bordering the Vogtland, a spring of salt issues forth; it contains sulfur and alum. If these matters were managed with understanding, the profit could be greater than most people currently believe.

## Concerning Hall.

Hall <note>referring to Hallein, near Salzburg</note>, the salt works under the Bishop, located two miles from the city of Salzburg, has several large *Rode* or *Golden* <note>archaic terms for brine reservoirs or extraction chambers</note> and possesses a fine, pure brine <term>Sole; naturally occurring salt water</term>. The pans used there are 24 feet <note>original: "schuch", literally "shoes"</note> long and approximately 22 wide; they boil continuously day and night and produce much salt. This is packed into small barrels, which are like half-herring barrels that have been cut in half through the middle, and it is called disk-salt <term>Scheiben-Saltz; salt hardened into round, flat cakes or disks</term>; they require no *Beisse* <note>original: "Beisse"; likely a reference to a mordant or additive used to clarify salt during the boiling process</note>. The salt boiled there is for the most part transported to Regensburg, Nuremberg, and Bamberg.

# At the Valley.
<note>German: "Zum Thal"; this refers to the modern town of Thale in Saxony-Anhalt</note>

In the County of Regenstein, located in the Harz mountains, a body of water flows out of the Harz called the Bode, upon which the Duke of Brunswick has established a timber float <note>a system for transporting logs downstream by water</note> so that firewood can be floated out of the Harz upon it. By this water, not far from a village called "At the Valley" <note>Thale</note>, a salt spring <term>Saltzquell; a natural source of saline water</term> emerges; it appears in a very narrow passage between two mountains, about 6 paces close to the Bode. It has a content of 4 *loth* <note>a traditional unit of weight, roughly 1/32nd of a pound or 14.6 grams</note> of salt per *centner* <note>a hundredweight, roughly 50 kilograms or 110 pounds</note>. There are three distinct wells encased in square frames, situated 8 or 10 paces close together. They are connected at the bottom so that they all come together into one well, over which a small house is built. It is lifted into a square tank, from which it is then conveyed further than two musket shots <note>a historical measure of distance; two musket shots would be roughly 400 to 600 meters</note> distance, closer to the village, onto a flat plain in

## Schönfeldt.

A salt spring breaks forth at Schönfeldt <note>likely referring to Schöntal near Bad Dürkheim</note>, not far from Dürkheim Castle on the Haardt <note>a low mountain range in the Palatinate region</note>; it contains two <term>Lot; a unit of weight, about 1/32nd of a pound</term> of salt for every <term>Zentner; a hundredweight, approximately 50kg</term> of brine <term>Sole; naturally occurring saltwater</term>. It lies under the jurisdiction and sovereignty of the Count Palatine, as the Elector of Heidelberg. This brine possesses a great sharpness <note>referring to a high concentration of minerals or acidity</note>, which is why it does not deposit any scale <note>German: "Stein," the hard mineral crust that usually forms on the inside of boiling pans</note> during the boiling process, as is the case in other places; it carries with it vitriol <note>sulfates of various metals</note>, saltpeter, and alum. A boiling pan was installed here some time ago, in the style and manner of those at Allendorf in Hesse, and of the same size, width, and length. The salt that is boiled from the brine is coarse and hard, like coarse bay salt <note>German: "Boy," a term for solar-evaporated sea salt often imported from France</note> or sea salt, yet it is white and pure, and very good for the curing of meat.

The salt, however, is sold at a high price, for not many salt springs are found in those regions along the Rhine River; rather, the inhabitants must for the most part be supplied with Hessian and

## Sulz

Sulz <note>likely Soultz-sous-Forêts in Alsace</note> lies approximately 6 miles from Strasbourg; there a salt spring emerges in the residential courtyard of a nobleman. A <term>Zentner; a hundredweight, approximately 50kg</term> of brine <note>German: "Sole"</note> contains one and a half <term>Lot; a unit of weight, about 1/32nd of a pound</term> of salt. This spring has not yet reached a state of consistent profitability. It carries with it a little alum, sulfur <note>original: "Schwebel"</note>, and saltpeter. And although the brine is of low concentration, the salt there is nevertheless sold at a very high price.

## Poserna in Meissen

The brine carries with it sulfur and saltpeter, and also a little alum; it is found in Meissen within the Electorate of Saxony. It lies in a very deep, mossy soil or marsh, so that for this reason, the brine cannot be easily captured. Indeed, the Elector of Saxony, Duke Augustus <note>Augustus, Elector of Saxony (1526–1586), known for his interest in technology and alchemy</note> of most honorable memory, spent no small <note>The text continues on the next page; catchword: "Vnko-" likely referring to "Unkosten" (expenses)</note>

## Lützen.

Not far from Merseburg near Lützen, a salt spring is located by the village of Dehlitz <note>original: "Deditz"</note>. A <term>Zentner; a hundredweight, approximately 50kg</term> of brine contains 5 <term>Lot; a unit of weight, roughly 15-16 grams</term> of salt. Recently, a <term>Strohleckwerk; a graduation tower using straw to evaporate water and concentrate brine</term> has also been established there. The spring carries with it nitre <note>saltpeter</note> and alum. The salt that is boiled there can certainly be used for food.

where the two waters from Vhrensthal and Armensi <note>Likely local names for Alpine streams in the Valais region</note> come together and form the Floß-Brunn <note>literally "Raft Spring" or "Flowing Spring"</note>, a salt vein or salt spring emerges. ## At Saint-Hippolyte in
## Burgundy.

At Saint-Hippolyte <note>original: "Sanct Polet"</note> in the Franche-Comté <note>original: "im Freyberge", referring to the "Free County" of Burgundy</note> in Burgundy, a salt brine is found which carries with it nitre, bitumen, and a little sulfur. A <term>Zentner; a hundredweight, approximately 50kg</term> of this brine contains <term>dritthalb Loht; two and a half lot (approx. 37-40 grams)</term> of pure salt. Every week, wood worth ten <term>Guilders; "Gülden", a gold or silver currency</term> is burned under one pan. Additionally, further expenses are incurred: <term>fünfftehalben; four and a half</term> Guilders for the laborers <note>original: "Gesinde"</note> who work there, as their wages, and four Guilders weekly for the transport of the salt. ## Werl. <note>original: "Werle"; likely referring to the town of Werl, a significant salt-producing center in the Archbishopric of Cologne</note>

A salt brine <note>original: "Saltzsola"</note> springs forth at Werl in the Bishopric of Cologne. By weight, one <term>hundredweight; "Centner", approx. 50kg</term> of this brine contains 8 <term>Lot; a unit of weight, roughly 15-17 grams or 1/32nd of a pound</term> of salt. It carries with it saltpeter and alum. There are 16 <term>salt-boiling huts; "Sölden"</term> there, and the pans in which the salt is boiled are about 14 <term>feet; "schuch", literally "shoes", a historical unit of measurement</term> long and 10 feet wide. This method of boiling has not been in use for long, for previously, five pans were placed one after another horizontally <note>original: "Wegrecht"</note>, each of which was 9 feet long and four wide, totaling 44 feet in length.

Because the new boiling works proved to be more efficient, the old works were abandoned. This same brine is not very clear before it is boiled down. However, when it is fully boiled, it becomes so pure and

### Unna <note>original: "Unaw"; referring to the town of Unna in Westphalia, a major salt-producing center that was part of the United Duchies of Jülich-Cleves-Berg at the time</note>

In Unna, under the Duke of Jülich, a spring flows that yields 3 <term>lot; a unit of weight, roughly 15-17 grams</term> of salt per <term>hundredweight; "Centner", approx. 50kg</term> of brine. It carries with it sulfur <note>original: "Schwebel"</note> and vitriol <note>original: "Victriol", a term for metallic sulfates used in various industrial processes</term>. There are three <term>production units; "Rode"</term> there, which use pans of the same size as those in Werl. However, a new boiling works was recently established there using pipes and other technical means; time will tell whether this will remain profitable. Otherwise, the brine is boiled into salt using wood and coal <note>original: "Steinkohlen"</note>. Furthermore, through God's blessing, a new spring was also recently discovered which contains 6 lot of salt per hundredweight of brine.

### At Koten <note>likely referring to Westernkotten, near Erwitte</note>

In the Prince-Bishopric of Paderborn <note>original: "Stifft Padelborn"</note> at Koten, there is a salt spring which provides brine from three different wells. ## Nieda <note>likely Nidda or Bad Nauheim in Hesse</note>

## Sulz in Westrich <note>Sulzbach in the Westrich, a historical region in the Palatinate/Saarland area</note>

At Sulz in Westrich, one league from Saarbrücken and belonging to the Counts of Nassau, a salt spring is found. It contains vitriol <note>sulfates</note>, sulfur, and saltpeter. A hundredweight <term>Centner; approx. 50kg</term> of the salt water contains very little salt, having no more than one lot <term>lot; a small unit of weight, approx. 15 grams</term> within it. The place where the brine is boiled is shaped like a smelting works <term>Schmeltzhütte; a foundry or furnace building</term>, such as is typically used for smelting ore. Otherwise, there is enough brine available. There is also pit coal <note>original: "Steinkohlen"</note> there, but because they have enough wood and no knowledge of the opportunity to use coal for salt-boiling, they stick to wood alone.

During the first boiling, the salt does not come out beautifully <note>meaning pure or white</note> because of the impurities in the brine.

Therefore, when the salt is first boiled, it is placed into a bag and leached <note>original: "durchgeleecket"; referring to a filtration process where liquid is allowed to drip through a porous material</note>, just as one is accustomed to leach lye. When it has been thus filtered and strained, the impurity remains behind in the bag. The pure liquid, however, which has passed through, is then put back into a pan and boiled once more into pure salt, which is thereafter sold for consumption.

### Roche in Switzerland <note>original: "Rotscha"; refers to Roche in the Canton of Vaud, famous for its historic salt mines</note>

In Switzerland at Roche, a salt spring flows through the earth, carrying with it sulfur, alum, and saltpeter—though only in small amounts. In an assay <note>original: "Proba"</note>, a hundredweight <term>Centner; a unit of weight approximately equal to 50 kilograms</term> of the salt water contains three and a half <note>original: "vierdtehalb"; an archaic German fraction meaning 3.5</note> lots <term>lot; a small unit of weight, approximately 15–16 grams</term>. The salt is produced and boiled in two sheds <note>original: "Koden"; regional term for salt-boiling huts</note>, in each of which there are two pans. ## Sana <note>This likely refers to Saanen in Switzerland or perhaps a variant spelling of Sulz, given the Württemberg connection mentioned</note>

Sana, under the Duke of Württemberg, is where one finds a salt spring which carries with it vitriol <term>vitriol; a sulfate mineral, often iron or copper sulfate</term>, sulfur, and alum. There is one boiling house there, in which there are two pans. ## Salins in Burgundy.

Salins in Burgundy <note>Salins-les-Bains in the Franche-Comté region</note>, belonging to the King of Spain <note>At the time of writing, this region was part of the Spanish Habsburg territories</note>. ## Salt Springs in
## Lorraine.

In Lorraine <note>original: Lottringen; a region in modern-day northeastern France</note>, the Duke maintains six distinct saltworks where very large boiling pans are used, measuring twenty-four feet <note>original: schuch; literally "shoes," a historical unit of length</note> long and twenty feet wide. At Rosières <note>original: Rosier; likely Rosières-aux-Salines, a major salt production center</note>, they burn nine cords <term>Claffter; a traditional unit of volume for stacked firewood</term> of split logs or thirty measures <term>Malter</term> of stump wood under a single pan in ten hours. From this process, six measures <term>Mutte; a historical dry volume measure</term> of salt are boiled, and each measure contains 16 smaller units <term>Sester</term>. The President governs the saltworks and collects the revenue from them. From 149 and a half hundredweight <term>Centner</term> and 36 pounds of brine <term>Sole</term>, 36 hundredweight of salt is produced in ten hours. The salt brine at Rosières <note>original: Rosier; Rosières-aux-Salines in France</note> contains alum and sulfur. The salt produced at all the saltworks in Lorraine can be used effectively both for food and for other industrial applications <note>original: Insatzen; likely referring to additives used in trades like tanning or dyeing</note>. 

This saltwork at Rosières is considered the smallest among the six saltworks belonging to the Duchy. There are two pans located there in a large boiling house <term>Sölden; a traditional term for a building where salt is boiled</term>. The brine is drawn from the well into a large wooden cistern using a horse-powered pump <term>Roßkunst; a mechanical system where horses walking in a circle provide the power to lift water or minerals</term>, from which it is then released into the pans.

Nothing but coarse split firewood is used for the boiling process, which is floated down a nearby waterway. This is the state of affairs regarding the Rosières saltwork. However, because Lorraine is not strictly included among the German salt springs, I have chosen not to provide any further account of the other Lorraine salt springs.

of Salt-Minerals.

# Where the Bay Salt is Re-boiled and Purified.

Bay salt <term>Boysaltz; a coarse, often dark sea salt produced through solar evaporation</term> is produced in Spain <note>original: Hispanien</note> and other places, especially on the island of Ibiza <note>original: Eivissa</note>, in large pits through the drying of seawater by the sun, and afterward carried by merchants to load their ships and brought to these lands.

This salt is black and coarse, yet quite salty; it is dissolved in other waters, and once the impurities have settled, the clear liquid is drained off and boiled again into salt and purified. This is now done in many places, but especially in the area of Botkyrka <note>original: Potticherland; likely referring to the Botkyrka district near Stockholm</note>, located half a mile from Stockholm near the dam, as well as in Enkhuizen <note>original: Genniessenseul; likely a phonetic rendering of Enkhuizen, a major Dutch salt-refining hub</note> in Friesland <note>original: Fruerschernland</note>, where the boiling is performed with peat <note>original: Dorff; an archaic spelling of Torf, meaning peat or turf used as fuel</note>. Bay salt is also re-boiled in Bremen <note>original: Brechmen</note>, as well as in Holland in Thionville <note>original: Diedenhoffen; likely referring to a specific refinery or an error, as Thionville is an inland city</note>, and in many other places.

The Third Part

# Concerning a Lake in Which the Sun Produces Salt.

In the wildernesses of the land of Podolia <note>A historical region in Eastern Europe, located in present-day west-central and south-western Ukraine</note>, and by the river Borysthenes <note>The ancient Greek name for the Dnieper</note>, which is now called the Dnieper <note>original: Niper</note>, there is a lake where the sun works the salt out of the water. It fills great pits, and once it is scooped out, it is poured into massive heaps. If the rain should catch or overtake the workers before they can move the salt away, it dissolves quickly, which occasionally ruins much of the harvest and causes great damage.

## Where Rock Salt is Mined.

Rock salt <term>Steinsaltz; salt that is mined from the earth in solid form rather than evaporated from brine</term> is found in Poland, near the city of Bochnia, four miles from Krakow. Likewise, at Wieliczka <note>original: Wilischka</note>, five miles from Krakow, the salt is extracted from the mountain at a depth of many fathoms <note>original: Claffter; a historical unit of depth, roughly 6 feet</note>, extending nearly half a mile underground. It is hewn into large pieces by the miners, which they call *bancos* <note>original: Bancos; likely referring to "banks" or large blocks of salt</note>. These are then moved from one shaft to another, as the shafts are sunk to various depths, as is the custom in many mines.

<summary>This page describes solar salt harvesting in the wilderness of Podolia near the Dnieper River and provides an account of the famous Polish rock salt mines at Bochnia and Wieliczka.</summary>

<keywords>Podolia, Dnieper River, solar evaporation, rock salt, Poland, Bochnia, Wieliczka, salt mining, Krakow</keywords>

commonly used and extracted, each piece of which is worth its own fixed amount of money.

Such rock salt <term>Steinsalz; salt mined in solid form</term> is also found in Transylvania <note>original: Siebenbürgen</note> in Szeklerland <note>original: Zekenland; a historical region in modern-day Romania</note>, as well as a league and a half from Hall in Tyrol. This is partly crushed small and used as is; some is first dissolved in water so that the impurities may settle, and it is afterwards boiled down again <note>a process of refining crude salt through recrystallization</note>.

In the salt mountains in Poland, one also finds a black substance like pitch or bitumen. From this, the Poles prepare a drink that opens and purges the body <note>a medicinal tonic used as a laxative</note>.

In the deep shafts, many kinds of strange voices have also been heard, such as roosters crowing, dogs barking, and even a roaring like lions and the voices of other animals, which is held by the people of that place to be an unlucky omen.

Various saltworks are also found in Ruthenia <note>original: Reussen; referring to historical regions in modern-day Ukraine and Poland</note>, such as at Halych <note>original: Holitz</note>, Kolomyia <note>original: Calomey</note>, Sohl, and many other places. However, because my primary intention has been to record the most prominent springs and salt veins of our Germany, I have not considered it necessary to describe distant foreign places with great detail.

At Obuerzin and in the Zips <note>a historical region in northeastern Slovakia</note>, salt water is also found, from which salt was boiled in former times and brought to the Polish court.

# The Fourth Part of this Book

## Teaches how one shall extract and prepare their salts <term>Salia; the chemical salts derived from substances</term> from all metals and the most principal minerals, as well as from animals and plants; also of their use, virtue, and power in medicine; and first, a short preface to it.

My dear reader should know that in this fourth part, not everyone will easily find their way, especially where metals and minerals are written of. For this requires knowledge, experience, reflection, and manual labor; and whoever is not versed in these, such terms <term>Termini; technical nomenclature used in alchemy and chemistry</term> will seem strange to him and will not be easily understood.

But those who have experience in chemical preparation <note>original: "Chymischer bereitung"</note> will know how to find the underlying principles, provided the work is correctly carried out and the practical techniques <note>original: "Handgriffe"; referring to the specific manual dexterity and "knacks" required in laboratory work</note> are properly applied. Which I did not wish to withhold from the favorable, kind-hearted reader as a notice for the introduction to this fourth part of my Book of Salts.

Take fine gold, which has been cast through antimony <note>Purifying gold with antimony was a standard technique; the antimony would bond with impurities and leave the gold refined.</note> three times, one part; six parts of quicksilver <note>original: "Mercurium vivum"; liquid mercury</note> that has previously been pressed through leather [to filter it]; make an amalgam from this. Into this amalgam, grind two parts of native sulfur <note>original: "lebendigen Schwebel" (sulfur vivum); natural, unrefined sulfur</note>; let it evaporate in subtle heat on a broad, flat roasting dish <note>original: "Schirbel"; a ceramic vessel used for calcination</note> under a muffle <note>A ceramic enclosure that protects the material from direct flame while allowing it to be heated by the surrounding air.</note>. Stir it constantly with a small iron hook, and do not give it too much heat, lest the material melt together, until the gold calx <term>Calx; the crumbly powder remaining after a metal has been roasted or oxidized</term> has become like a beautiful yellow marigold-colored flower; then it is prepared correctly.

Thereafter, take one part saltpeter, also one part sal ammoniac <term>Salarmoniacum; ammonium chloride, often used as a flux or to create strong acids</term>, and half a part of small, ground, pure pebbles <note>Silica from pebbles was often added to such mixtures to prevent the salts from melting into a solid mass and to facilitate the release of acidic vapors.</note>. ### The Fourth Part

...[I] did not want to leave [this] out / And the way to prepare it is thus:

Take one pound of a good **royal water** <term>Aqua regis; a powerful acid mixture (literally "royal water") capable of dissolving gold, usually made from nitric and hydrochloric acids</term> made by means of **sal ammoniac** <term>Salarmoniac; ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemical processes to strengthen acids</term>; dissolve eight **lot** <note>a historical unit of weight, roughly 1/2 ounce or 15 grams</note> of sal ammoniac in it. Put them into a **flask** <note>original: "Kolbenglas"</note> and pour the royal water upon them, and let it dissolve as much gold as you can possibly dissolve therein. When all the gold has been dissolved, pour a little **oil of tartar** <term>Oleum tartari or Weinsteinöhl; a concentrated liquid form of potassium carbonate used to neutralize acids</term> into it; or **salt of tartar** <term>Sal tartari; potassium carbonate in dry form</term> dissolved in a little well-water and poured in—it does the same thing. It will then begin to hiss and effervesce <note>original: "braussen"; the chemical reaction produces bubbles as the acid is neutralized</note> greatly. When it has finished effervescing, pour more of the oil in; do this several times, and as often as needed, until all the dissolved gold has fallen to the bottom out of the water.

When that also is done, pour the royal water off from the **gold calx** <term>Goldkalche; the fine powder or "ash" of gold remaining after chemical precipitation</term> and **sweeten** <note>original: "süeß"; to "sweeten" or edulcorate means to wash the substance repeatedly with water to remove any remaining salts or acids until the wash-water is tasteless</note> it with common water eight, ten, or twelve times in the very best manner

Afterward, when the **gold calx** <term>Goldkalck; the fine powder or "ash" of gold remaining after chemical precipitation, specifically "fulminating gold" in this context</term> has well settled, then pour the water off from it and dry the **gold calx** in the air where no sun shines, and certainly not over the fire. For as soon as this powder feels even a very slight heat or warmth, it ignites and causes remarkably great damage; for it would depart so fleetingly with such great violence and power that no man could restrain it <note>This is a description of Aurum Fulminans (fulminating gold), an explosive compound that can detonate with friction or slight heat</note>.

Now, when this powder is also ready, take a strong distilled **vinegar** <term>Essig; acetic acid, used here to stabilize the volatile gold compound</term>, pour it over [the powder], and boil it steadily in a large quantity of the same, stirring it constantly so that it cannot settle on the bottom, for twenty-four hours continuously; in this way, its "striking" <note>its explosive or detonating quality</note> will be taken away from it again. But take great care and use great caution so that you do not fall into danger through any oversight. Then pour the **vinegar** off from it, wash the powder well again as before, and dry it once more.

This powder can be driven **by means of an alembic** <term>per alembicum; a distillation process using a helmet-shaped apparatus to capture vapors</term> without any **corrosive** <term>Corrosiff; a harsh chemical solvent or acid used to break down substances</term>.

## How the Salt of the Moon, or the Salt from Silver, is to be Made.

But the <term>residue</term> <note>original: "residuum"; the solid material left over after a liquid has been filtered or evaporated</note> / or what remains behind / when the vinegar will no longer color itself / take that entirely dry by itself <note>original: "per se"</note> / and pour a honey-water upon it / as mentioned before / which was made through distillation / but let it stand in the warmth / so that the honey-water extracts the salt into itself.

When that has happened / then pour the water off clearly / so that the <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "feces"; the sediment or waste matter</note> remain behind / and distill the water from it / thus the salt remains at the bottom <note>original: "in fundo"</note> / wash away the sweetness well through frequent repetition of the distillation / and clarify it with <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>original: "spiritum vini"; high-proof distilled alcohol</note>, then it is finished.

# Now follows further the method / how one shall make its salt from the seventh metal, mercury or quicksilver.

Take <term>living mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurium vivum"; liquid metallic quicksilver</note> or <term>quicksilver</term> / from it make a fragrant <term>oil</term> / which <term>mercury</term> has first been <term>sublimated</term> <note>original: "sublimirt"; a process where a substance is heated into a vapor and cooled back into a solid to purify it</note> seven times / and <term>revivified</term> <note>original: "vivificirt"; restored to its liquid metallic state from a compound</note> again by means of quicklime <note>original: "lebendigen Kalch"; calcium oxide, used as a chemical agent</note> / upon the remaining <term>earth</term> <note>original: "Terra"; the solid residue left over after distillation</note> pour the <term>oil</term> / which has been made from the <term>mercury</term> / or the <term>star or spirit of mercury</term> <note>original: "astrum sive spiritum Mercurij"; the inner celestial essence or highly refined volatile portion of the metal</note>, which because of the great heat maintains its own <term>body</term> <note>original: "corpus"</note> in a steady flow / place it in the warmth / thus the <term>oil</term> draws its own <term>salt</term> / which it had left behind / into itself / When that has happened / then pour a fair portion of <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>original: "spiritum vini"; concentrated distilled alcohol</note> into the <term>oil of mercury</term> <note>original: "oleo Mercurij"</note>, <term>distill</term> it all together in the ashes <note>referring to an ash bath, used to provide gentle, indirect heat to a glass vessel</note> / so the <term>oil of mercury</term> passes over after the <term>spirit of wine</term> / and the <term>salt</term> remains behind / which is <term>resolved</term> <note>original: "resolvirt"; dissolved or reduced to a liquid state</note> in new <term>spirit</term> / and through frequent drawing-off

# The Virtue and Power
### of each Salt of the Metals / how they may be usefully applied to Medicine and the health of man / learn now from the description mentioned below.

## First, concerning the Salt of Gold.

The <term>Salt of Gold</term> is used in small quantities / one or two <term>grains</term> <note>original: "gran"; a traditional unit of weight based on a grain of cereal, roughly 64.8 milligrams</note> in a spoonful of good <term>aged wine</term> <note>original: "firnen Weins"; wine that has been stored and matured for at least a year</note> / it allays all ailments / from wherever they may come / strengthens the heart / renovates and renews the blood / makes the heart joyful / dispels <term>melancholy</term> <note>original: "Melancholiam"; in the 17th century, this was viewed as both a physical humor and a state of depression</note> / creates a good memory / strengthens the brain and all limbs / breaks the <term>bladder stone</term> <note>original: "Blasenstein"; kidney or bladder stones</note> / dispels leprosy / and opens the hardening of the joints / which is called <term>podagra</term> <note>original: "Podagram"; a form of gout, specifically painful swelling of the foot or big toe</note> /

### Of the Salt of Mars

The salt from <term>iron</term> <note>original: "Eyfen" (Eisen); iron was alchemically linked to Mars, the god of war, and was believed to possess strengthening and binding qualities</note> is good for the "red flux" and illness called <term>dysentery</term> <note>original: "roten Ruhr... Dysenteria"; a severe inflammatory disorder of the lower intestinal tract resulting in bloody diarrhea</note>, taken with wine vinegar and a little <term>oil of vitriol</term> <note>original: "Oleo vitrioli"; alchemical term for concentrated sulfuric acid, used here in a highly diluted medicinal form</note>. Its weight [dosage] is 5 or 6 <term>grains</term> <note>a historical unit of weight approximately equal to 65 milligrams</note>, according to the person’s condition. This salt also strengthens the stomach, for it helps one to digest very quickly; it stills <note>original: "stillet"; meaning to stop or regulate an overabundance of flow</note> the women’s time, called <term>menstruation</term> <note>original: "Menstruum"</note>, as well as all abdominal fluxes and <term>diarrhea</term> <note>original: "Durchgänge," literally "passages"</note>. Mixed with <term>virgin honey</term> <note>original: "Jungfraw-honig"; the purest honey that flows naturally from the honeycomb without being pressed or heated</note>, it draws out all <term>abscesses</term> <note>original: "Apostema"; a localized collection of pus or swelling</note> and poisonous boils, and cures them thoroughly.

->Concerning Mineral Salts<-

...with spirit of wine <note>original: "spiritu vini"; distilled alcohol or ethanol</note>, it is good for those who are moon-struck <note>original: "Lunaticis"; individuals believed to be influenced by the moon’s phases, often referring to those with epilepsy or mental illness</note>, and it drives away melancholic thoughts from Saturnine people <note>original: "Saturnischen Menschen"; according to the theory of temperaments, those influenced by the planet Saturn were thought to be prone to gloom or depression</note>. Dissolved in a little fennel water and placed in the eyes, it brings good vision, consumes the superfluous phlegm <note>original: "flegma"; one of the four bodily humors, associated with coldness and moisture</note> and moisture therein, and is useful for all evil, moist, putrid, and old corrosive sores <note>original: "vmbffressenden Schäden"; spreading ulcers or gangrenous wounds that "eat" the surrounding flesh</note>.

## Concerning the Salt of Mercury.

The salt of mercury <note>in alchemy, the metal mercury or quicksilver is symbolized by the planet Mercury</note> is taken in very small quantities; its highest weight for strong persons is two grains <note>a grain is a traditional unit of weight, approximately 65 milligrams</note>. Where the nature [of the patient] is weak, however, scarcely one grain is used. Taken with a spoonful of the water of Guaiacum wood <note>original: "aqua ligni Cuaici"; a decoction made from a tropical wood known as "holy wood," which was the primary treatment for syphilis in the early modern period</note>, it drives out all the "French disease" sores <note>original: "Franzosen Schäden"; a common historical term for syphilis</note>, regardless of how large they may be or how long they have lasted. It renews the entire blood and all afflictions that cause putrefaction <note>original: "Faule"; internal rot or decay</note> from which leprosy <note>original: "Aussatz"</note> and all manner of mange <note>original: "Reudigkeit"; itchy skin conditions or scabies</note> might arise. It also dries out the ringworm <note>original: "flechten"</note> in the hands and all skin eruptions <note>original: "Massern"; likely referring to measles-like spots or pustules</note>.

## How one should make the Salt of Antimony.
<note>original: "Sal Antimonij"</note>

Take good Hungarian **stibnite** <term>stibnite: the primary ore of antimony, known in German as "Spießglas"</term> / grind it very small / fine like a flour / and **calcine** <term>calcine: to heat a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to remove volatile components or oxidize it</term> it over a gentle charcoal fire / as one is accustomed to do, constantly stirred with an iron rod / until it has become quite whitish / and no longer smokes / but can finally endure a fairly strong heat / Then put it into a **crucible** / set it in the fire and melt it / so that a beautiful transparent yellow glass is made from it / Grind this glass very small again / put it into a glass flask / and pour a good strong distilled wine vinegar upon it / let it stand thus / in gentle warmth / so the vinegar draws the **tincture of antimony** <note>original: "tinctura Antimonij"</note> into itself / and colors itself quite deeply / which tincture or extraction of antimony <note>original: "tinctura oder extractio Antimonij"</note> can afterwards be further prepared / and used in other ways with great and excellent benefit.

### Part Four: USE

The weight of this **salt** is set at three <term>grains</term> <note>original: "Gran"; a very small unit of weight, roughly 0.06 grams</note>. It should be taken in a dose of one <term>quentin</term> <note>a small weight, roughly 3.7 grams</note> of the **balsam of sulphur** <note>original: "Balsamis sulphuris"</note> that has been prepared with **oil of juniper** <note>original: "oleo Juniperi"</note> or **spirit of turpentine** <note>original: "spiritu Terebintinae"</note>, after it has first been **sublimated** <note>a process of heating a substance into vapor and collecting the purified solid residue</note> through **iron oxide of vitriol** <term>iron oxide: "Colchator victrioli," the red residue remaining after the distillation of vitriol</term>, **aloes** <note>original: "Aloepaticum"</note>, and **myrrh**. This is taken for a third time with the addition of **Oriental saffron**, and it drives away all ailments of the lungs.

Those who have **shortness of breath** and are **narrow-chested** <term>narrow-chested: "Engbrüstig," a historical term for asthma or respiratory constriction</term> should use this salt dissolved in its own **balsam**; they will find speedy improvement. # The Fourth Part

### of Salt-Minerals

Whoever desires to have a clear voice should take half a **quintlein** <note>original: "quentlein," a historical unit of weight approximately 3.6 to 4 grams</note> of this saltpeter and stir it into an egg yolk from which the white has been separated. Drink one every morning on an empty stomach. He will maintain a good voice or recover one that was lost, provided it was lost only **through** hoarseness.

Take of this saltpeter, one part **salt of wormwood** <note>original: "Wermuht / Saltz," likely the alkaline salt produced from burnt wormwood</note>, two **loth** <note>a unit of weight approximately 15 grams</note> of each; one **loth** of white **amber**; and twelve **loth** of white **loaf sugar** <note>original: "Hut-zucker," sugar refined into a hard, cone shape</note>. Mix five or six drops of **anise oil** into it. If you are able to obtain **oil of amber**, however, it is better than the amber itself, as it is easily acquired and simple to prepare. 

If this is taken three or four times every day—each time three good **knife-tips full**—and used for a month or longer, it expels all phlegm and impurity from the bladder. It also breaks apart a stone that has begun to form; but if it has already reached the stage of <term>Coagulation</term> <note>the process of thickening or hardening into a solid mass</note>, it drives it away without the need for any **cutting** <note>original: "schneidens," referring to the surgical removal of bladder stones, a dangerous procedure at the time</note>.

### The Fourth Part

One takes saltpeter and salt in **equal parts** <note>original: "ana"; a medical term meaning equal quantities of each ingredient</note> and melts them well together; afterward, pour it out, grind it small, and place it in the cellar to **dissolve into water** <note>original: "resolviren"; this describes deliquescence, where a substance absorbs moisture from the damp cellar air until it turns into a liquid</note>.

Then take one part copper sheet and one part sulfur, **layer them** <note>original: "stratificirs"; to arrange in alternating layers</note> together, **cement them** <term>cimentirs: a process of heating substances in a sealed vessel to change their chemical nature</term>, and make **burnt copper** <note>original: "æsustum"; also known as *aes ustum*, a scorched or oxidized copper used in early chemistry</note> from it, as is the custom. Afterward, grind the burnt copper small, and to every pound of the material add 8 **loth** <term>Loht: an old German unit of weight, roughly half an ounce</term> of additional sulfur.

Grind them together and **calcine** <term>calcinirs: to heat a substance to high temperatures to oxidize it or drive off volatile parts</term> it finely and gently so that the material does not melt. When the sulfur has all smoked away and burnt off, give it a fairly strong fire and pour it into warm water; in this way, the **spirit** <note>original: "spiritus"; the essential or volatile essence</note> of the copper and sulfur passes into the water.

Afterward, pour off the water and dry the material that remains at the bottom. Add new sulfur to it—8 loth to one pound of the material—and proceed as before. Repeat this until the water has become completely blue; then the **properties of Venus** <note>original: "Venerische eigenschaff"; in alchemy, Venus is the name and symbol for copper</note> have been drawn into it and resolved into a **vitriolic nature** <note>original: "Victriolisch wesen"; referring to the creation of blue vitriol or copper sulfate</note>, which will also be found...

Of this water take two **stübchen** <term>Stübichen: an old German liquid measure, roughly equivalent to 3.5 to 4 liters</term>, of the previously **dissolved saltpeter water** <note>original: "resolvirten Salpeterwassers"</note> one stübchen, three stübchen of **spring salt brine** <note>original: "quellende Saltzsohle"; natural saltwater from a spring that has not yet been boiled down to salt</note> which has not yet been boiled, throw in a handful of **salt of tartar** <note>original: "Sal tartari"; potassium carbonate, often derived from wine lees</note>, and three pounds of crushed **alum**, and let it dissolve; pour 18 stübchen of common water into it, and the bath is ready.

If one bathes in this several times, as warm as it can be endured, twice every day for two or three hours, it helps those again who are completely **stiffened** <note>original: "Contract"; a term for limbs that have become paralyzed, contracted, or drawn together by illness</note>, drives away the **mange** <note>original: "Räudigkeit"; a general term for skin eruptions, itching, or scabies</note>, and restores lame limbs to health; it makes the veins supple and draws to itself all **coagulated matter** <note>original: "coagulirte Materia"; thickened humors or fluids that early physicians believed caused blockages and stiffness</note> which has caused the hardening; it serves to treat **gout** <term>Podagra: a historical term for gout, specifically painful inflammation of the joints, often the big toe</term> and performs everything that any other warm or natural thermal bath

# Plume Alum, or Feather-white.

This is a **fixed salt** <note>original: "fixes Saltz"; in alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that can withstand fire without evaporating or changing</note>. Internally, little can be written of its virtue. Externally, however, it dries out all **evil fluxes** <note>original: "böse Flüsse"; harmful discharges or "humors" flowing from a wound</note> and ailments of wounds when strewn therein. It stills the bleeding of wounds and the **joint-water** <note>original: "Gliedwasser"; likely referring to synovial fluid or a serous discharge from a wound near a joint</note>.

## Borax.

This salt is not **corrosive** <note>original: "Corrosivisch"</note> as other salts are, but is a **sweet salt**. Externally, this salt is not used particularly much. Internally, however, it causes a **binding effect** <note>original: "Verstopffung"; while this usually means constipation, in a medical context it refers to the astringent property of "stopping" a flow or discharge</note>. Therefore, take one **quint** <term>Quent: an old German weight, roughly 3.6 to 4 grams</term> of **Saffron of Mars** <note>original: "Crocum Martem"; a chemical preparation of iron oxide used for its strengthening and astringent qualities</note>, one quint of **nutmeg** finely crushed, one quint of **Sealed Earth** <note>original: "Terra sigillata"; a specialized medicinal clay stamped with a seal to prove its authenticity, highly valued as an antidote and astringent</note>, **pomegranates**, and **carob** <note>original: "Johannesbrot"</note>, likewise of each as much finely crushed, and with unsalted butter bake a small cake thereof, with an egg, quite dry, and give it to the sick person to eat. This prevents the **breakthrough of the salt** <note>original: "Durchbruch des Saltzes"; likely referring to an excessive purging or a caustic reaction in the body</note>. The sick—

### Of the salt that is drawn from Sealed Earth, and first how such is to be made.

Take <term>Sealed Earth</term> <note>original: "Terra sigillata"; a medicinal clay, often from the island of Lemnos, stamped with a seal to certify its authenticity as an antidote and astringent</note>, well-refined and washed so that no sand remains, and dried again. Rub it fine and pour <term>Spirit of Turpentine</term> <note>original: "Spiritum teribintini"</note> over it. Let it stand for two months to **digest** <note>to extract or soften a substance using gentle, consistent heat</note> in steady warmth, so that the **Spirit** draws the best strength of the earth into itself. Pour off the <term>Oil</term> cleanly, and **reverberate** <note>original: "reverberir"; to heat a substance in a furnace where the heat and flames are reflected (reverberated) onto the material</note> the remaining earth for five days and nights continuously with an open flame. Then, rub it fine and **extract** the **salt** with distilled, corrosive honey-water. It should be sweetened **by distillation** <note>original: "per distillationem"</note> and purified with **spirit of wine** <note>original: "spiritu vini"; a high-proof alcohol or ethanol</note>. If the earth is good and strong, one pound yields one <term>quarter</term> <note>original: "Ort"</note> of a <term>quint</term> <note>original: "Quent"; an old German unit of weight, roughly 3.6 to 4 grams</note>. Put this same salt into a good Venetian <term>Theriac</term>, <term>Mithridate</term>, or **Golden Egg** <note>original: "Tyriack, Midritat oder gülden Ey"; "Theriac" and "Mithridate" were famous ancient "universal" antidotes made of many ingredients. The "Golden Egg" refers to a specific prestigious medicine, likely the *Ovum Aureum*, often used to treat the plague.</note>
